"Co away, sir. Don't you know better than to disturb me when lam writing. Now that idea is gone, and I must begin all over again. Thereis- no-placte—' " It was a woman's voice this time: "Reginald, the gas is leaking like everything, and you must see the company and have it stopped." "There is no place like home," began the wretched man again, just as Hannah, the girl, thrust her head in to ask: "Did you order the kindlin's ?" Then he seized his pen and wrote fluently for full five minutes before he gave up his wild dream of fame, "There is no place like horns—for discomfort, annoyances, cold, hunger, and evert -land of dis- turbance on the fare of the earth, at the most in- opportune and trying moment." SOME CAPITAL THEATRICAL ANECDOTES. MIS- OELLANEOCS Gossip.—Harry Hudson, who is this season playing John Murray with the "Money Mad" Company, is an old-time actor who dates his recollections of the stage from Forrest and Edwin Adams. He has supported all the great • stars for the last 25 years and tells many an amus- ing anecdote about them. A number et years ago he was a member of a stock company in Baltimore, whea C. W. Couldock came there to pay a round of the legitimate. In " Richelieu" Mr. Hudson was cast for Louis XII. and in the fifth act was guilty of a lapse of memory which brought down torrents of wrath upon his head from the irascible Couldock. During the great scene where the king discovers the treachery of Baradas and his brother -Orleans, he rushes over to the dying cardinal and exclaims: "Live, Richelieu, live ; If not for me, for France !" " For France!" Richelieu replies, and the thought of his country seems to give him new life. On the night in question Mr. Hudsen made one of those mistakes that may happen to theinotit careful actor " Live, Richelieu, live " he exclaimed. "If not for France, for me!" Couldock turned on him like a whirlwind and shot at him a glance that was meant to transfix him. " YOU ! he roared at the top of his powerful voice: "who the devil are you ? ' A SEAMAN'S WlT.—There is a certain naval officer who has on certain occasions come very near rendering himself immortal as a wit, although those who know him best say that a sense of humour is not his most conspicuous quality. He is a carpen- ter by rank, and as such it is his duty to sound the ship's well at eight o'clock every evening and report the depth of water in Lb,: bilge. One night. when the ship was cruising near a rocky coast, the sounding rod revealed about two feet of water swashing about the bilges. The carpenter made note of the fact, and shortly after he had reported the result of his investigation the engineer started the steam pumps, and in a few minutes the ship was pumped dry. An hour or so later the ship went ashore, and in the excitement and confusion that followed, the first lieutenant found time to send a messeeger to the carpenter, ordering him to .sound the well immediately and report. The car- ' penter dropped the sounding rod into the tube, and, drawing it up again, looked in consternation at its unwet surface. " Whew :" he ejaculated, "worse than I thought." " What shah I tell the first lieutenant aaked the impatient messenger. "Never mind; I'll report in person," said the car- penter, and, climbing up the bridge where the executive was shouting orders, he solemnly im- parted the information that the ship had a big hole •stove in her bottom. "Great Scott ! is that so?" exclaimed the officer, as he countermanded an order to sew up a sail and gave another one to stand by the I:oats. "True as you live," said the car- penter. There wi ts e feet of water in the bilges just now, and she must have been stove in, for every drop run out." After this effort the car- penter's associates were quite prepared to ly-ar him tell a boat's crew one day that let wantal to " hear them all keep perfect silence."

"CAPITAL" CHAT.

The enormous success of the great temperance demonstration and the ezicotraging replies of §:dr W. Harcourt and Mr. Gladstone to the dePutatron have directed attention again to the Local Veto Bill. There is undoubtedly a stronger feeling •in favour of pressing Sir. W. Harcourt's bill than there was last spring and summer. The contention of ,the Alliance that the bill pure and simple will be of the greatest value as a Parliamentary acceptance 3f the principle of local option has gained ground, and titers are fewer temperance men now who argue as to the probability that the bill would not be put in operation in most parts of England. Some prefer that the third alternative, given in the Welsh and Scotch bills,of a reduction in the number,of licensed premises should be inserted:hi the English bill as a provision likely to have immediate practical results and consider that with the eating-house clause the Veto Bill will be too easily evaded. But generally opinion gathers strength that it will be well to pass ' the bill through the Commons before the general election

There is, however, a growing feeling that there is a special danger in any such legislation if it does not at the same time deal with the urgent question of clubs. In every industrial district working men's clubs, so called, ate springing up like mushrooms, and only a small fraction of the number can be classed as" bogus 'clubs. At these working men's clubs in many towns liquor is sold and consumed or carried away at any hour of the night and through- out Sunday. It is not only the " bogus" clubs run by individuals, but also bona-fide combinations of working men, which thus evade the restrictions of hours under the Licensing Acts. It is argued that the moment there is a prospect even of local option being carried out these clubs will still further multiply, and reform be made impossible. It is not unlikely, , therefore, that .this question -will be brought to the fore by private members next ses- sion in the interests of temperance.

Louis Kossuth, the Hungarian patriot, quickly acquired a wonderful command of the English language, as was shown by his public speeches dur- ing his celebrated tour in this country. Sometimes however, he was at a loss for a right word. Once he spoke of "taking time by the air,' an noticing d smile going around, turned mutely to a gentleman on the platform behind him, and he suggested " by the forelock." Another instance is recalled by the "Boston Transcript:' Speaking at Concord, Mass., Kossuth wished to express the figure of the Aus- trian eagle rending the young freedomof .Hungary. The word escaped him. Stopping for a moment in the full flight of eloquence, he asked a matter-of- fact American who sat near bim, " What you say when man tear his coat ? " " Hole," was the reply. That word did not satisfy him, and Ralph Waldo Emerson, who had overheard the question, whis- pered, "rent," with poetic sympathy for euphony, and the stately sweep of the sentence was coin- , pleted.

M. Casimir-Perier, the new French Premier,is the kind of man who may dazzle the imaginations of susceptible French politicians. He domes from a family of statesmen—his grandfather was Prime Minister for a short time in the reign of Louis ' Philippe, and his father filled the office of Minister of the Interior under Thiers ; be has the reputation of being as. wealthy as Crcesus ; he is magnificent in his hospitality ; and he'is known to be a hero. In the Franco-German War he was a captain of Mobiles. During a hot fight his chief was shot down; and young Casimir-Feder daringly dashed forward and rescued the betty from under a storm of shots, lie was decorated for his gallantry. Thht decoration has gained him many electoral victo- ries. _

Gal•saii The organ of Anarchism in England is "Freedom." It could hardly be described as a flourishing news- paper property. The price is a penny, and it is only published once a month. Naturally it does not attract advertisers, and, in fact, not a single adver- tisement appears in it. In all probability the profits do not represent an appreciable percentage on the exceedingly small expenditure. Nevertheless this tiny sheet is rather interesting. It is edited, pub- lished, and almost, or perhaps quite, entirely written by a gentle, refined, and cultured lan ~once a Newnham student, whose friends would find it impossible to imagine any cruelty or brutality in her nature. Obviously this lady is entirel?puzzled by the recent Anarchist outrages. In the current number of her paper she discusses the question of homicidal outrages by those whom she affects to represent. She says that Anarchists " attach a peculiar sanctity to human life," and also look " with sheer horror upon every destruction, every mutilation of a human being, physical or moral." But "the conscientious Anarchist " has Parlia- mentary agitation barred to him. She then, how- ever, asks whether homicide is" the necessary anti- thesis "to the usual means of agitation. Here the lady becomes vague. She recalled the outrages of the Italian, Irish, and Russian revolutionists, and suggests that human beings may be "goaded and driven by sheer desperation into conduct obviously viobnitly opposed to their social instincts." This nonsense apparently satisfies the writer, or she pre- tends that it does so.

The Labour Commission sat eight hours on Tues- day, with a brief break of half an hour for luncheon. The Duke of Devonshire presided, and there were twenty-two members in attendance. The sitting was altogether occupied with the consideration of the review of the evieence as drawn •up by the secretary. Before the sitting a private exchange of views to,,k place between the Labour members, with the result that the intention was abandoned of challenging the construction of the report, and it was agreed instead to criticise the secretary's dralt as submitted, with the view of presenting a minority report embodying their separate views ow specific points. The recommendations to be drafted by the chairman were not forthcoming on Tues- day, and until tney are submitted the Labour re- presentatives will withhold their own views. This course was suggested by Mr. Tom Mann, as enabling them better to formulate other recommendations. • The Commission adjourned at its rising to the 22nd of January, when the chairman's recommendations should be submitted. The report cannot now ap- pear before Easter next.

Mr. Gladstone looked very feeble on Wednesday on his arrival in London. He has not been able to take his usual outdoor exercise at Brighton owing to the stormy weather, and although be has quite recovered from his recent bilious attack he is not at all strong. He still takes very much to heart Sir Andrew Clark's death, and also the suicide of his valet Outram, who, it is now known, was under re- straint, owing to the advice of Mrs. Gladstone, for a month previous to his disappearance. Yet Mr. Gladstone has all the fire of an old man's energy, and will ,meet what is left by influenza of the Cabliet in Downing-street to consider the naval ' question, flung by the Opposition as a red herring across the path. That he is not to be hurried into anything like a heroic naval programme 1 am told is certain, but there is to be shipbuilding, steady and ' sure, though not of the panic order.

At the meeting, on Wednesday, of the Labour Commission it became more evident than ever that a strong minority report is inevitable. The Duke of Devonshire does not seem to have secured the confidence of the advanced members of the Com- mission, and the fact that he is entirely in the hands of Mr. Drage, the secretary, who is suspected of reactionary tendencies, does not help to improve matters. Mr. Tom Mann, Mr. Tait, and Mr. Austin are among those who are opposed to the lines sug- gested for the drafting of the final report.

The Freedom Group of Anarchists—the most res- ponsible of all the groups into which English An- archism is divid 11—has now, I learn, declared em- phatically against homicidal outrage as an instru- ment of Anarchist propaganda. In fact. it has laid it down that the man who would commit murder in cold blood, no matter what the circumstances, is a homicidal maniac ; further,that to inspire another to commit a murder is a crime. These views will, it may easily be understood, scarcely be acceptable ,to all the groups, more particularly to those with which the foreign element is associated. It is be- lieved, however, that the policy thus declared must make way, and its authors are intent on pushing it by lectures to be given at all the Anaraists resorts here.

THE EXAMINER. SATURDAY, DECEMBER 16, 1893. LADIES' LETTER;

14 DEAREST NELLIE, The most charming colours are in fashion this winter for young girls, who no longer seem to think it necessary to appear only in white. White satin, however, is worn by them before the hour in Iwhich they stand lit the hymeneal altar, and a lovely gcwn is of white satin worked in gold thpsad, with turquoise stones at intervals and lauds of beaver fur, the sleeves being of jurquoise blue satin. This gown for an older married woman might be made in violet ve vet, intermixed with the eagle turquoise blue, the blue satin being introduced underneath the gold and the fur at the hem. It has elements of most of the novelties of this season's the evening gowns, such as the flow of drapery at the foot produced by the upper skirt being slashed to show the frill ; the pleated basque, the stiff straight bodice, the puffed sleeve, and the cape which falls rather at the side of the sleeve, than over the puff. These points are all elegant, new, and becoming ; and while the style is simple, it admits of the richest ornamentation.

Amongst the prettiest of the prey iashions in seasonable head-dress is a lovely toque made in shot miroir velvet in a lovely shade of heliotrope. The hat is smartly trimmed with sable.coloured ostrich feathers, brush aigrettes to correspond, and small sable tails most artistically arranged.

When you are judging black silk pull out a thread of the filling an ascertain if it is strong. If it stands the test, then rub one corner of the silk in the hands as though washing it. After this operation, if it be good silk, it will, upon being brushed out, look as smooth as ever. If on hold. ing it up to the light and looking through it, you see no traces of the rubbing, be sure the silk is good. The warp and filling of a good piece of BM does not differ much in size; should it differ, ths silk will not wear well. If you choose a figured silk, let the figure be small and well woven' else it will soon become frayed and have a Bhabb3 appearance.

A short cape, but sufficiently long tot hide the waist and to keep the lower arms warm and cosy, is lined with fur. The best part of the liaing of an old—or half-worn—fur cloak would be just the thing for the purpose. The model is of cherry red cloth, edged and trimmed with chinchilla, but astrachan would do equally well, and be more economical wear, as chinchilla though becoming and exceedingly fashionable, is nevertheless a very delicate fur and one quite unfit for daily use. Being so small, the above cape should be made in a bright shade. Scarlet serge would look delightful for skating and country wear, but would be.hardly suitable for towns. Artificial flowers are now worn on evening gowns, placed as if they were growing at the side of the skirt, but nearly always intermixed with ribbons. They are introduced with a lavish hand, large bunches, and large trails having the pre- ference, and only the most perfectly natural flowers are now used. In Paris, one bloom with leaves tied with ribbon is placed at the side of the bodice, and the same flower with leaves falling on either side, a bow of ribbon in the centre, is a moss fashionable sort of aigrette; but then French women dress their hair so that these spiral ribbons nestle in among the curls or coils. We in Englana seera to prefer a rouleau of velvet or ribbon with a bow in the centre and upstanding ends like ear,. among which the jewels are interspersed.

Amongst the wedding dressing I saw recently, very prettily trimmed were the bodices, with folds of velvet narrowed into the waist, alternating with soft frills of lace, lace edging the décolletage, while the back of the bodice showed a novelty in a. fully pleated basque terminating on each hip and made of mature velvet.

The festive season is now close upon us, and you are doubtless racking your brains for ideas, and your scrapbags for pretty remnants, wherewith to make presents, and I thiuk a few hints on the sub- ject of inexpensive trifles may be useful. A novelty which looks attractive hung on a tree is the Christmas boll cushion. To make this, two pieces of stout cardboard must be carefully cut in the shape of a bell (the size depends on the amount of material at hand to cover the cushion), and one piece is plainly covered with silk, satin, or even sateen, this being for the back, the front section, which is similarly covered, rtquiring a slight padding with cotton-wool or coarse flannel to give it a rounded appearance. The two sections are then neatly oversewn together ; but before the join is complete, a bit uf narrow riobon is inserted at the top of the belt, and at the bottom a large bead is attached to a bit of flue co d, and fastened. so as to represent the tongue or clapper. Pins those with coloured glass heads look prettiest—are stuck all round between the two attetions of card, and the pincushion is faesteued by the piece of rib- bon at the top to a cross bar of card covered to match the bell, on which a UONY of ribbon and a loop to suspend the cushion are as .10. This cushion can be ornamented in various ways ; covered with white satin, and painted with a spray of holly, it looks very pretty, or "A Glad Yule,' or some similar greeting, may be painted or worked on it, or it may be embroidered with the monogram or initial of the person to whom it is given.

Yours lovingly, JENNIE.

Lady Salisbury had three eucalyptus plants in her room after Lord Salisbury had influenza, and no one took any infection. - •

The Belfast papers announce the deaths of two women named Watson and McGee. aged respectively one hun- dred and four and one hundred and six years. "

A society has been formed in China, called the "Heavenly Foot Society," which is attempting a crusade against the inhuman practice of binding the feet of women.

It is, quite the thing kr smart Society women to play at shop-keeping nowadays. There is Mrs. Franklin, whose nom de comtnerce is Madame Vera, and Mrs. Menzies, who trades under the name of Madame Marion. Mrs. Charles Pen boldly displays her own name over the dooe of her establishment ; and Lady Granville Gordon is professionally known as Mdme. Lierra. The Bishop of Wakefield, in opening an art exhibition in BuCkingham Palace-road, ,congratulated the ladies ' ilpressent on having discarded the fashion of heaping up theie hair like a Tower of Babel, and having reverted to the more artistic Grecian style of wearing the hair low down upon the neck. With regard to ladies' dress, the prevalent shoulder shapes suggested that they were cul- tivating-wings. A writer in a magazine speaks highly in praise of the_ Maoris. The women, he says, are a potent, factor, in island life, and must not be forgotten. Liquid dark eyes, soft voices, affectionate and merry dispdsitions, light brown skins of ivory smoothness, and gracefulness in every movement, are their most striking characteris- tics. The men add women are graceful, it is said, ' because of the prevalence of dancing and bathing. In these supersensitive days,remarks,Mr.Vincent in his life of the Duke of Clarence, only the sons of men of high rank are subject to wholesome discipline. Thus a peer at Eton or Winchester may be, and sometimes is, flogged with a severity which, if shown by a Board schoolmas- ter to 'an artisan's son, would be followed by a scene in a police court. So much is this the case that the Bishop of Chester observel not long ago that the privilege of being flogged in youth was being confined to the rich. Lady Brooke, now Countess of Warwick, is probably one of the best known society women of the period. She is beautiful and has gracious wanness. Lady Brooke is a splendid huntswoman. She. hunts in pink, and as she is invariably splendidly mounted and rides as straight as a die, she is generally the most conspicuous figure in the field._ Lady Brooke is an enthusiastic horticulturist, and she possesses a knowledge of the subject which many a gardener might envy.

Lime and Lime-water. , The uses of so homely an article as lime about the household are almost innumerable—just the ordinary lime that one sees the plasteryr and bricklayer ure in preparing his pacts of 'plastei• or mortar. Even when reduced to an almost ianpalpablesolutin in water, it is still efficacious in a remarkable variety of instances. One sees the hodrnen on a new building keep their drink ing water in a pail that is coated with lime, and one thinks that it is a poor receptacle for the universal bev- erage. Yet it'would not be so good or so pure served in a silver ice-pitcher. The lime-water of the druggists is indeed nothing more than this solution of the hodmen. A piece of unslacked • lime in a perfectly clean bottle, with cold water poured nver it, tlte bottle corked and kept in a cool, dark place, is a full recipe for lime-water It is ready for use. in a few 'moments after the water is poured on. This lime-water; has a number of uses,' as well as the lime,, itself. A spoonful of it in a cupful of milk is a remedy for the summer complaint common to childreu. It is admirable for cleansing nursing bottles. It corrects acidity of the stomach. It will prevent the , "turning "of milk or cream, and a cupful added to bread 'sponge will keep it from souring. Allowed to ; evaporate troinat vessel over the stove, its steam proves a.wonderful alleviation for the distresses due to lung fever, croup or diphtheric ailment. • It will sweeten and,' purify bottles, jugs, kc., and lastly, a few spoonfuls of it poured on potted plants will rid them very effectually of pot worms. Lime itself, as every one knows, is Inval- uable as a purifier and disinfectant. Sprinkled in cellars or closets where there is a slight dampness, it will not only serve as a purifier, but it is a valuable preventive against the invasion of noxious animals. In greater cases, suchAs the disinfection of vessels and hospitals, it is recognised as a chief agent. It is one of thc notable instances of the economy and bounty of nature that this article, so cheap and common is serviceable in so many Instances.

Christmas Plum Pudding. " The Table" gives the following, amongst other I ' original recipes, for making a plum pudding for twelve ; persons :—One pound of finely grated bread crumbs; one pound of muscatel raisins, stoned and halved ; one pound of best currants, waslud, picked, and dried; one pound of sugar, pure cane; three-quarters of a pound of beef suet.eboppecl fine; two ounces of each of orange, lemon, and citron peel,cut in nice strips ; two ounces of almonds, chopped fine ; a saltspoon of salt, and a little nutmeg ; mix these together. Whisk five eggs,to which add two dessert•spoons of brandy, mix altogether, and put into a buttered mould or basin and tie with a cloth; if not mdist enough add a little treacle, boil or steam for four hours, and put away for a week or two, then boil or steam foi two hours more. I prefer them steamed, as they retain their flavour better and eat much lighter. —Five eggs, two ounces of flour, quarter of a pound of bread crumbs, half a pound of suet, six ounces of moist sugar, half a pound of currants, half a pound of raisins (stoned), quarter of a pound of chopped mixed peel, quarter of a pound of mixed crystallised fruits chopped, quarter of a pound of almonds cut in strips, quarter of a pint of brandy, quarter of a pint of sherry, one salt spoonful of grated nutmeg, half a saltspoonful of salt. Mix all thoroughly together, pour into mould, and boil for nine h.tirs. Keep about a month, and boil again for two hours when required for use. For Christmas turn out, put a piece of frosted holly on the top, pour brandy over,and set on fire.—One pound of chopped beef suet, three-quarters of a pound of stoned raisias, three- qua' ters of a pound of cleaned and picked currants,half a pound :.•.f fine flour, half a pound of breadcrumbs, quarter of a pound of moist sugar, quarter ot a. pound of cliopped Apples, quarter of a pound of chopped mixed candied peel, one teaspoonful of mixed spice, the.grated rind of three lemons, four whole eggs, half a pint of sweet ale, and a glass of brandy. Mix altogether and steam in a basin or plain mould for five hours. When the podding is dished pour a small glass of brandy over it and send it to table burning. Also serve iced brandy butter in a boat.

PARTED BY FATE;

OR THE MYSTERY OF BLACK-TOR LIGHTHOUSE.

BY LAITEA JEAN LIBBEY,

Author of " lone, the Pride of the Mill," &c., &O.

CHAPTER XXI. THE FATAL SECRET THAT SHADOWED 17LDENE'S YOUNG LIFE. This was Tuesday. The stranger would not find out the name of the beautiful girl whose face had attracted him until Thursday. How could he re- strain his impatience until that time? the stranger asked himself as he paced up and down his room in the hotel in the greatest of excitement. "The face is so fatally like, she must be her child," he muttered, his face darkening and his eyes flaching. "I remember it was a girl." He turned from the window, again pacing the room with hurried strides, as if to keep pace with his thoughts. One could see at a glance that he was a foreigner —evidently a Frenchman—a tall, dark, handsome man, with a proud, resolute face, which wore at all times an aspect of almost fierceness. It was a face that was seared with a story, as though it had been burned there with hot irons. There were great Ibles of horrible pain around the restless, dark eyeas,which the brooding shadows never left, that told'of sleepless nights and wretched days. "The eighteen years is almost up,' he muttered, with a bitter imprecation: "and so help me Heaven, when the time is come I shall nlaz the whole story to the world 1 Oh, Uldeue ? Uldene !" he cried, "you might have made a friend of me, but you chose to make me your bitterest enemy I How dared you allow your child to marry, though she were as beautiful as an houri I • Ah, you knew why she should not, yet you did not warn her. "She must part from the man she has wedded, as you, before her, were parted from your love. Yes, she must put the whole world between herself and the man she loves, if she would escape the fatal curse of the daughters of her race." Early on Thursday morning the stranger pre- sented himself at the academy, and received the desired information. The lady's name was Mrs. Uldene Chester, and she lived at No. A— Avenue. "Uldene !" he muttered as he turned away. "Ah ! I was not mistaken, then. It is her child! It must be! The name is not a common one. And her face I Ah I who could not trace a resemblance In the girl's dark face"? It maddened me as I looked at it--set my brain and my heart on fire. "How was he to gain an interview with this girl?" he asked himself, as he stood motionless before the number indicated, and looked keenly at the house far back on the green, slowing lawn. Opening the gate noi,selessly, he entered the grounds, made a circuit of them, finding hinisvlf In the beautiful flower garden at the rear of the house. It was not an enviable position if he were to be detected there by any of the servants. Still he was one of the coolest and most daring of men. He would be equal to the emergency. While he was planning a way to the furtherance of his object, fate favoured him unexpectedly. He saw the side door that opened out upon a porch suddenly open, and a slender, graceful figure emerge from it, cross the porch, and stroll (town into the grounds. Unconsciously fate guided her footsteps in the very path that led to the spot where the stranger stood, well concealed by the shadows of the rose arbour. It was Uldene. How wondrously beautiful she looked in the bright glow of the moonlight. She was robed in a dress of soft, clinging, fleecy white, with dark red roses on her breast, and roses in her dark, curling hair. Diamonds glittered in the pretty, pink, shell-like ears, on the white bands, and ran like a river of glittering Pre around the white, perfect throat ; but they could not outshine the glittering splendour of the starry eyes that sought the dark night sky so wistfully as she walked slowly along. "How foolish I am to give way to nervous fears and presentiments," she cried, half aloud. "I will forget the dark, haunting face that has made even my dreams horrible. Why should not Ihe happy? I hitve Retledge's love, why should I not be happy, I ask myselt again? I do not believe' the bitterly cruel story Mark Set ton wrote to Rut- ledge's mother. I am almost eighteen, and the fate that was predicted has not overtaken me—no sword has fallen upon my head." The moonlight lay white and silvery on the dew- steeped flowers; the night wind stirred the leaves of the roses, and their odour seemed to float around her and enfold her. There was no sound save the twittering of the birds as they sought their nests in the popular trees ; nothing else broke in upon the sweet broodisg silence of the night. And in that fatal hour, when the birds twittered, the moonlight fell peacefully on the roses, and the light of Heaven seemed fairest, beautiful, hapless Uldene's doom fell upon her. A dark shadow fell over the scarlet blooms, looming up darkly between her and the moonlight; and glancing sip, with a low cry, she beheld a man standing in the path before her. In a flash she had recognised him as the stranger she had seen in the picture gallery a few days before, and whose face, waking or sleeping, had been before her ever since. "Hush "he cried, springing forward. "Make no outcry, as you value your peace and safety— aye, your very life !lam no robber—no intruder; I have been searching the earth over for you for- nearly eighteen years, and I have found you—at last Uldene." She recoiled in anger and dismay too great for words. Who was this man who dared address her I thus familiarly? Who was this stranger who forced himself into these grounds to accost her? She raised her voice to call the servants to her assistance that they might summarily eject him, but he anticipated the movement, and caught her white arm in a clasp that made her wince with pain. "You would rue it to the day of your death, girl, if you were to summon your servants, or per- haps your husbantl here, and they should hear what I have to say to you. Your ears, and yours alone, must hear what I have to tell." "You are certainly a madman," cried Uldene, indignantly, struggling to flee herself from the strong, steel-like clasp that held her white arm fast. "You shall judge of that later on," he said, quietly, adding, with an intense bitterness that quite frightened her: "Your mother made a relentless foe of me in years gone by; see that you do not do the same, for I otn crush you, or save you from a fate more bitter than death, if I so will it." The words he uttered held her spell-bound. They froze the piercing cry on her lips, made her reel dizzily forward, like one about to swoon. She would have fallen but for that steel-like clasp that never loosened its hold of her white arm for a single instant. Quickly, and in a low, hoarse voice, he began his story —the startling revelation which was to burn its way to her brain, and cause her to cry out to Heaven for mercy, or death. An hour passed. A horrible hour that had been counted by the girl's spasmodic heart-throbs as she listened to one of the bitterest, cruellest revelations that ever fell upon human care and broke a human heart,

CHAPTER XXII. "CRUEL FATE HAS PARTED US, DARLING." " I know that the sentence of death is written

Against our love by the hand of fate; I know all the joy of our lives is smitten, A deadly blow by the direst hate; "But my heart rebels with fiercest passion; It will not submit to their stern decree; Love cannot be slain in mutErrous fashion; Like a giant, it struggles for mastery. " Though they bury it deep in their deadly malice, Crushing out, as they think, its latest breath, Yet, with strength renewed, as from blood-fille

- , chalice, - It will rise to prove that love knows no death. "If love is a clime—a sinful passion— Why, then should a God so good and wise Have moulded our hearts in such wondrous fashion That nothing but love e'er satisfies?

" If it be a sin, and only repenting And firmly forsaking brings pardon divine, Then my soul must be doomed to woes unrelenting, For no change can e'er come to a love such as mine !" The death-like swoon into which Uldene had fallen was of short duration; with a shock, memory returned to her. She sprang to her feet with as bitter a cry as ever fell from mortal lips, and gazed fearfully around her. Her enemy was not there; but within the hour she knew he would return for his answer; nothing could prevent that. And then—ah, God help her / She dared not think what would happen then. An hour! She had an hour's respite. What might not be accomplished in that length of time! Ships had been wrecked, yet whole crews had been saved in half that length of time; floods had covered whole villages, but in the hour the water was rising the people had fled to the hills and saved themselves. Whole cities had been swept by flames, and the people had saved themselves in the first hour the alarming warning had been given. Why sholild she not make the most desperate effort of her life to save herself in this fatal hour? Alti if she could put the whole world between herself and her merciless foe—flying, not alone, bat defying fate itself by taking Rutledge with her ! "Oh, my love, my love," she moaned, "in spite of all, how am I to part from you?" If it were left for her to decide, her great, passionate, blind love would influence her. She stood in the long, dew-wet grass, holding out her little white hands to the stariemmed sky ; and only heaven knew the struggle that was going OD in her soul, for she knew there would come a day when the curse of God would fall upon her, as it had fallen upon every daughter of her fated race, if, after this warning, she did not part at once and forever from her love ere it was too late. "Oh, God, teach me how I am to give up my love whom I love so well I" moaned Uldene. "Oh, white clouds 1" she cried, "choose for me! I ask you to decide a human life—a human soul to-night. If yonder fleecy clouds obscure the face of the moon as they pass, I solemly pledge to you that within the hour I will part from my heart's love, mAking no moan, though it will be the bitterness of death for me. If, on the other hand, yonder clouds sail by, and no shadow drifts over the moon, I will cling to Rutledge's lover, defying fate itself. I pledge myself to Heaven that those white clouds shall decide for weal or woe, for joy or the cold. nese and darkness of despair." Never in this world was there a more piteous look than the beautiful young face turned upward to the moonlit, star-gemmed sky, and the white clouds that were to decide either life or death for her. She knelt down in the dew-wet grass, with her ice-cold hands clasped over her heart, piteously watehing the white clouds. Nearer—nearer they approached the great white light. Uldene caught her breath in a panting gasp. Did the angels realise her woe? Would they intercede for her, begging that mercy might be shown her? She was so young, and she loved him so. One instant more, and they would decide her future. That instant seemed the length of eternity. Nearer, nearer they approached the soft, bright, silvery orb, and as she watched them, slowly but surely they obscured it. Ah, yes ! it was heaven's solemn warning to her that she must part from her love, if she would save him. In that moment the girl turned away with the bitterness of death in her heart, and passed slowly up the lilac grove, sweet with the breath of purple, tossing plumes, to the house. She had thought to gain her own room without being observed; but this was not to be. In the corridor she came face to face with Rutledge. "I was just about starting out through the grounds in search of you, dear," he said. ' What were you doing out among the roses so long ?" He started abruptly as he saw her face. It was as white as marble, and the large, dark eyes had in them a hunted look, dazed by tenor. "Uldene 1" he cried, spring to her aide, and throwing his strong is about the slender, sway- ing figure, "what s the unttter, deer? Are you 11114

THE JOYS OF CHRISTIIAS CAROLLING.

SOME LOCAL EXPERIENCES.

BY LITRE BLUNT.

Yes, I suppose there is a great deal of joy and happiness attached to Christmas carolling. But I have not yet quite made up my mind where the joy comes in ; although I'm inclined to think it is when the cadences of the music are mellowed by I distance, say, for instance, when the carollers are pouring out their f ull-souled happiness about ten or twenty streets away. Contrary to the assertion contained in many books on national customs—that carolling" is quite a recent institution "—I am prc- pared to say that its origin is not of quite so recent a date. But Ido not wish to take any credit for this discovery; it is riot pleasant to be flattered for every little common-place service rendered to u- Manity. I made the discovery by .picking up a book 1 in the library. It contained the announcement that Christmas carolling (said to be derived from •cantarc, to sing, and role an interjection of joy ) is of very ancient date," It further stated, on the authority of Bishop Taylor, that the " Gloria in Excelsis "hymn was the first carol ever composed, and that it" was sung by the angels to the Shep- herds at the Lord's Nativity." I never like to boast about my historical knowledge, bat 1 am prepared to prove that according to the facts quoted, carol- ling must have been in vogue some time before the Jubilee year. tly opening assertion also finds cor- roboration because the first quotation states that ',rolling is derived from rola, meaning an interjec- tion of joy. Bet it is to this Joy I wish to allu,e,

A few years ago in the unregenerate days 'EnY youth, II joined a band formed with a charitable object. As a band of musicians We may have been of doubtful quality; but "we were a decided success, the very acme of perfection, as a band of eccentric "night howlers." 'The challenge is thrown to anyone who cares to refute this. Voursubscriber was appointed treasurer. This was not because he possesses any special qual- itiesas a financier, but simply because he could •only perform on two instruments, the cymbals and the-Jews' Harp, and that it was •unnecessary for ' him to struggle with these difficult instruments. the band made obvious, by ,unkindly asserting that they were not essential to the sweetness of the performances. One night about a week before Christmas, the " institution " assembled, chiefly Hi uniform, but. I regret to add, mostly in spiritual exhileration. When d raised remonstrance about the latter characteris- tic was informed that musical .geniuses had to be so, before they could do good work. But, I 'don't believe this now—not after the Work turned out that night. We first visited houses in town, to -which.places we had thoughtfully sent post cards, informing the occupants of our intended visit. One of my duties was to knock at the door after the Performance, and to present the contribution plate. The first.place we called at was not a success. The , occupant 'interrupted us in the middle of the first carol by asking if we were the band. "We have that honour "I said, when the instru- Ments were all quiet. "Oh, well, l'm very sorry—l'd quite forgot—but I intended -sending word that it would not be con- , Nenient." I *as about to make some explanation, when the i •occupantgraciously bowing to us, said.: ".1 wish YOU a very good evening ;'' and then retired in 'doors. The scant success was not repeated at the next two places. But at the third it recurred again. ,Built almost against the wall of the house we this time 'visited, was an arbour, covered with decayed creeping,plants. Into this place we marched, and, having seated ourselves, the performance was com- menced. It occurred to me that a little vocal accompaniment would improve matters, so 1 broke out into the carol," Hark, the Herald Angel sings." •Jobson who was playing an accordion—not quite in accordance with a general wish—also joined in 'the vocal accompaniment and the shock nearly took away my breath. • His voice was just" break- 'hie and, whilst he would attack the low motes in .a dejected .sort of bass' his voice, when the high notes were reached, would fly off at the handle and •break into a discordant cackle and shriek. Pre- aently I said, with a certain tenderness of tone.: le'a`r.j.obson.l" ,But he was so engrossed he did not I •repeated, louder-"Jobson, old mac:" But Continued:: " Hark the Herald Angel sings." "I say, Jobsori" . "Glory to the ne-ow bormEi-ing." ". Doti't yeas, hear:" "Tara rar,•ta.ra rat rarra " (his substitute for !forgotten words). Look here, why the deuce don't you sing pro- Terly. Don't go and improvise both words and lime, please." " Who ! me'l ate out of But at that 'mement a window creaked. Then a 'harsh female voice broke ouG in the night air.;. '"Scats you---:" But the end of the senterce•was 'drowned in a splash ot water which rushed through the arbour on to the disconcerted instrumentaiists. !Like one man, the •whole band bounded into the Toad, It was then discovered that Timkins—evid- 'ently the greatest genius as he had imbibed•tlD- -3110st freely—had left his fiddlerin the arbour. consented to return with him. He moved aloag etealthily on his toes.; every step he took he raised tie knee almost to his chin, andtapped his arms allabing the snostaxaggerated pretence at caution': Re seemed to be imitating somer trapper he had read of, tor about every three steps he would put L ulB nger to his lips and say; "Slit Shi ! There's maybe a dog abed' ,(hio) sornewheresh." However, we succeeded in rescuing the instrument all right.

The next Tifit was to the house of a certain Rentleman in NT;altou. He had been notified by t'osteard. Maybe be was not pining to hear us; Lit still, we meant to allow him no chance to fret lh out Qur absemee. The ;route chosen, lay across r rPIeY, and over Morley Commr•n. When we "ached the centre of the latter place, someone ?Pied a iarge lantern being.conveyed slowly along Lae high road, apparently near the Brewery. It owas at once surmised that this was the Ilantern of eur oPPosients, the --land. They ,too, were ,v_idently making for the same house as ,ourselves. lat2rget who proposed it. But I remember that in monaents,we were all running at full.speed to '_'rur_ destination. Having reached the place, three ° four carol,s were hastiiy galloped through. Then otrang the _ bell; a valet came, and asked the name rf the band, and after onveying it to his c .master enturned with half a sovereign. So I said: "Play another tune," and Vilen I thought of a way to get out of the scrape. urriediy striding up the steps to the door, I said, aloud .“ very sorry!, But good night," and then " Well boys my friend's away from home." Said Jobsou: "But no one came to the door." "Did I not &ay. sir, that my friend was away." " Yes, but no one else did.' Then he caught sight of the wet) viaows and examJned a,dngs, and said:

"Well, here's ago ! Playing for twenty minutes at an empty house !" Well, that carol curled itself up there on that top note, and died. I was checked and rebuffed, atm Jobson said if I hadn't lied, it would not have been so bad. I said I did not lie. "You did. You said some one came to the door." "Sir, I deny it. I said my friend was away from home. That was all. You know he is—you all know, so where is the lie ?" But the harmony of the proceedings was totally wrecked,and they actu- ally seemed to think I did lie, whereas, I was, per- haps, goiag to think about doing it ; but I didn't.

- However, the end soon came. Proceeding down the laue towards the . lodge, we came to a bramble bush on the road side near the brook. It wasmoori- light, and some one proposed a leap over the bramble bush. Ticklebotham clewed it, 80 did Thompson. Then came Jobson. He alighted on the top.; and a serious mishap happened to his pants. But while he was discovering the damage done, Timkins essayed the leap.; stumbled over the hush, and rolled into the brook, which was several inches deep with mud. It wouldn't be wise to detail al that was said.; it would not be,proper, because the language would not -embellish anyone's‘vocabu- , lary, although it might enrich their store of invec- tive. When Timkins was scraped, he said:— 'lf this is carolling, I've finished?' , Said Jobson, who had been compelled to borrow I a long overcoat to render him a less interesting I figure.: "So'vell. It's simply beastly. 'You don't catch me at it again:" Some one asked who had proposed the leaping first, and each one accused the other, with the re- sult that in a few :moments, matters had arrived at such a pitch that it was impossible for us to remain together longer. We despised each other—and all despised carolling. The band had been started for the purpose of assisting a charitable object.; but it never assembled again. And with this experience in mind I wish to emphasize all that has been said about the joys of carolling.

THE MUSICAL SOCIETY'S ,CONCERT.

The Warrington Musical Society entered upon its ,present season's work under disads antageous cir- cumstances in regard to a room in which to hold its ,performances. With the old-Public Hall (now the -Royal ,Court Theetre) no longer available, the choice was narrowed to two rooms, the Gymnasium and the Drill Hall, neither of which are.adapted to the Society's requirements. We understand that the ,Officers of the Volunteer Regiment having kindly placed the Drill Hall at the service of the-commit- tee, and other circumstances tending to a decision in that direction, this offer was taken advantage of. It was in the Drill Hall, therefore, that the con- cert—the.first of the season—was given on Tuesday evening. Much work in, preparing, decorating and heating the building bad been seen to be.neceesary, and these arrangements—so vital to the comfort of both performers.and audience—had been caret ally carried out, and proved. in. every way eatistactory. " Samson was the work, chosen tor performance, and, in essaying this, perhaps the most dramatic of flandel's oratorios, the Society joined forces with the Runcorn Musical. Society. Taking advantage of the fact that Mr. Crossley. is now tee conductor of both the Warrington and the Runcorn societies, a performance by the united members seemed a fitt- ing method of dealing with so grand a work. And the ssquel-demonstrated the advantage to be de- rived trom this course. A mass of vocalists and instrumentalists some-250 in number were thus asso- ciated, and a really.fine performance was the result. Returning the compliment to the Runcorn contin- gent, the oratorio was repeated ou Wednesday at Runcorn, under precisely the same conditions, much .to the delight, it may be surmised, of the local lovers of music. Our own pertormance, so far as the two societies Are concerned, was a most :creditable one. There were, of course, some imperfections. The worst was when the tenors and altos missed the lead on the re- currence of the subject of the..first chorus. And not until the second part of the evening, did the members of the chorus seem thoroughly to settle down to their work and to sing with that spirit and " go " which they then sliewed that they had in reserve. But after all is said, there weresome excellent points in the singing of the chorus. The .massive grandeur of many of the choral members had been clearly recognised, and wheo, now and then, some .contrasting passage afforded the needful scqpe, there were some fine subdued effects aud stifle delicate touches of vocal colour. After the little mishap to which reference has been made, and when the chorus had ,pulled themselves together, capital pre- cision was shewn in the entry of the various parts, and throughout.the,concert all the voices were shewn to be admirably under .the control of Mr. Crossley's baton. The principal vocalists were Miss.Tnudichum, Madame Berteushaw,, Mr. James Gawthrop, and Mr. Bantock Pierpoint s and no better a quartet need tie desired. It is something of.a misfortune in this oratorio that no ver7 " taking" numbers fall ..to the lot of either the soprano or the contralto. .IsTevertheless ". Let the Bright Seraphim" must certainly be noted as a gem to wheel Miss Thudichum imparted a brilliant lustre. And simi- larly a fall measure of true devotional feeling was evinced by Madame ,Bertenshaw in "Return 0 God of Hosts," which, to our mind, is one of the finest movements in the oratoric. The tenors great opportunity is, of course, when in the character of Samson he laments his blind- ness in " Total eclipse." And, occurring early in the course of the oratorio Mr. Gawthrop here made an excellent impression. -Subsequently, however, and not- ' ably in some of the _recitatives, and in the duet " Go baffled coward ".in which he is associated with Harapha, he tailed to rise to the occasion his declamations of defiance being both weak and _ineffective. In the duet with Miss Thudichum " Traitress to love" there was a much more equal-balance-of the parts, the interweaving nt the animated ,phrases of this number giving quite is realistic effect to the domestic wrangle expressed in the text. " Thus when the Sun "was feelingly sung by Mr. Gawthrop and next to " Total eclipse" was unioubtedly his best effort. Mr. Bautock Dierpoint reerivtd quite an ovation, and in two numbers autithe- .cal in sts le and Iseatment, he-won demands for repeti- ins which were, perhaps, the highest compliment a delighted audience could pay him. "Honour and Armes " and " How willing my paternal love," are in stuM direct contrast as to form a fairly good test of an attitre's versatility end all-round ability. The orchestra —which it is pleasing to note was for the most part -made up of local instrunientalists—left little to be .desired. It was in every respect adequate and efficient, .and,its performance saf the Overture, the Dead March, and the dramatic touches of tone colour depicting the .;atastrophe, was a source of general admiration. Mr. J. 13—Caldwell ably undertook the onerous task of accompanying the numerous recitatives on the American "An. Teem was a large attendance.

WIDNES TOWN COUNCIL

The monthly meeting of the Widnes Town Coun- cil was held in the Town Hall, on Tuesday after- noon. The Mayor (Alderman F. H. Gossage) pre- sided, and there were also present, Aldermen Ware- ing, Timmis, and Tilley, Councillors Quinn, Owens, O'Donnell, Gough, Singleton, Gossage, Smith, Shut- t leworth, Neil, Barlow, Bullough,W right, Hodgkin- son, Davies and Longtoa, and Messrs. H. S. Oppen- beim (town clerk), T. Craik (deputy clerk), S. , Boyle (accountant.),l. Carr (engineer),and W. Rowe (nuisance inspector). HIGHWAY COMMITTEE. This Committee reported that Alderman Sadler had been elected chairman and Councillor Christie deputy- chairman for the ensuing year, also that agreements with Mr. A. Palin for improvements near to Sutton's- lane, and with Mr. W. Smith for the improvement of Albert-road and Sadler-st.' was approved of. It was re- solvedthat the tender of Mr. W. IL Johnson for the supply of hay and straw for a period of three months at the prices quoted, be accepted. The Surveyor re- ported that a complete overhaul of the steam roller was required, andthe tender of Messrs. Gilchrist and Ce., had been accepted for the required work. A large num- ber of streets had been repaired. The 24in. pipe sewer in Cromwell-St. and Marsh-st., were nearly completed, the work having been carried on by relief labour, 162 men having been employed on an average of two days each. The following plans submitted by the Surveyor and approved.:—Alteration to shop in Waterloo-road, for Parr's Banking Co. ; two cottages Castle-st., for John Hunt ; additions to Cosmopolitan Hotel, Victoria- St., for A. Brouchemite ; alteration 13, Water-st., for F. Lawrinson. The Surveyor submitted plan and estim- ates for the flagging of Albert-road, Halton View, and Moss Bank. Resolved—That the same be approved,and that application be made to the Local Government Board for sanction to the borrowing of £1,382 103. 3d., the amount estimated to be required. Accounts amount- ing t0.£3,242 6sswere examined and passed tor payment. Estimate of wages required for the next four weeks £3.1.8 per week,wa,s approved and referred to the Finance Corn-I mittee.—The confirmation ofthe minutes was moved by Alderman Sadler, seconded by Councillor Neil, and car- ried unanimcusly. 'rue births registered were 132 or 39.6 per 1,000. There were 64 cases of infectious diseases re- ported. The Market Inspec- tor reported that the receipts for the month had been , 210 3s. 51., as compared with £l6 4s. 2d. for the cor- responding month of last year. The Surveyor submitted plan and estimate of proposed disinfector and mortuary at the Infectious Diseases Hospital. GAS AND WATER COMMITTEE.--COST OF THE . COAL

DISPUTE.

A meeting of this committee was held on December 5, when Alderman Timmis was elected chairman, and Alderman Wareing deputy chairman. Reports of tests made by Mr. C. E. Bellamy, of Liverpool, as follows, read : on the 18th and 30th Oct., illuminating power, 15.49,and 17.02 candles respectively, and on the 13th and 28ta Nov. : illuminating power. 20-28 and 19 94 candles respectively. The delivery of coal under contract was resumed on the 22nd ult., making a period of seventeen weeks since the supply was stopped. The quantity of coals purchased outside the contracts for the gas works amounted to 2,850 tons, at an average cost of 16i. 81d. per ton. The contract price being 6s. 10d., the extra cost had thus been 9s. 10id. per ton, making the total extra cost of coals £1,405. To this, however, must be added £2OO, the estimated amount of loss sustained through the decreased consumption of gas, making the total cost of the strike at the gas works, say, £1,600 or 2 82d. per. 1,000 cubic feet en gas sold. The effect of the strike upon the water works has been even more serious than that of the gas works in consequence of the large decrease in the consumption of water by the works, which alone amounted to a total loss in revenue of £l,BOO. The extra cost of fuel had been £314, making the total loss on the water works £2,114. The total loss in both woiks thus being £3,714. 'Relief to the unemployed had ceased since the termination of the dispute. The total number of men dealt with was 320.—The adepti‘n of the minutes was moved by Alderman Timmis seconded by Councillor Barlow.--Carried. GENERAL PURPOSES AND PARLIAMENTARY COMMITTEE . He hoped that the amendment would be withdrawn, Coun- cillor Davies declined to withdraw, and the amendment being put was lost by a considerable majority.—The minutes of the committee were then passed. Ac- counts amounting to £5ll 15s. 4d., examined and passed for payment.—The minutes were approved on the motion of Councillor Wright, seconded by Coun- cillor O'Donnell. - THE PROPOSED COMMISSION OF THE PEACE FOR WIDNES. In accordance with a notice of motion, Councillor O'Donnell moved that the Council petition Her Majesty tZe Queen to form a separate commission of the peace for the borough of Widnes. He understood that when a petition for any borough was sent to the Queen for this purpose it had scarcely ever been refused. Ile did not wish at present to go in for a separate police force. Of course if they had a separate commission, they would require to have separate officials which would however only make the eost about £3OO in round figures. To meet this, they had school board cases. About 336 persons were prose- cuted in the year by the school board who had to pay 4s. 6d. for each summons. Then there was the county fund, If they had a separate commission, this fund would come to the borough. Added to these there were the private summonses. Making a rough calculation, he considered that the extra expenses and the income would be about tialanced. Councillor O'Donnell next gave a number of instances of boroughs less than Wid- nes who had a separate commission. He also pointed out the paucity of magistrates near Widnes. Councillor Gough seconded the motion. He would like to see a separate commission for Widnes, so that work- ingmen magistrates might be appointed. If they looked at the justices who appeared at the Widnes court they would find scarcely any who knew the conditions under which the people of Widnes lived. He did not hink that the country squire abould come to the town to try the Gown people.

1H E AIVIINER, SATURDAY, DECEMBER 16, 1893. IMPERIAL PARLIAMENT.

The Mayor said that he did not see any advantage to Widnes in having a separate commission of the peace. He considered thAt the magistrates knew the circum- stances of Widnes very well. Councillor Smith also defended the present magistrates and considered that it would be a great loss to Widnes if they altered the course in which they were at present proceeding. Alderman Wareing asked if it was not the case that the Mayor and ex-Mayor were magistrates by virtue of their office at the present time. The Town Clerk replied that they were,anel the Mayor had precedence of the other magistrates in local cases. Councillor Davies said that he thought there ought to be magistrates appointed who lived in Widnes such as Major Wareing. But with regard to the motion of Councillor O'Donnell, he censidered that Widnes was a baby corporation not much over twelve months old,and now it wanted its separate commission of the peace. He thought that it was scarcely time for that yet, and should vote against the motion. Councillor Quinn said that he had a motion somewhat similar to Councillor O'Donnell's, which he had with- drawn. He had withdrawn that motion because he was not satisfied that there would be no more expense, and until he could be assured that there would be no extra expense, he should oppose the motion. Councillor Hodgkinson advocated a stipendiary in con- junction with other boroughs. Councillor O'Donnell in replying to the various speak- ers, said that not one of them knew what he was talking about. He was proceeding to taunt Councillor Quinn with being bought over, when that gentleman rose and indignantly repudiated the assertion. The Mayor inter- posed and both gentlemen resumrd their seats.—Upon the motion being put to the council, only Councillors ' O'Donnell and Gough rose in its support, and it was accordingly lost. Councillor O'Donnell next moved that the Chairman of the General Purposes Committee supply information as to the police duties in Widnes.—Councillor Gough seconded.—Alderman Wareing said that it was impossi- ble to comply with the motion, as the committee were not in possession of the information.—Upon the motion deing put, only the proposer and seconder voted for it. This concluded the business of the council.

RUNCORN 'COMMISSIONERS AND INFECTIOUS DISEASE.

The Local Government Board forwarded a communi- cation which they had received from Runcorn Improve- ment Commissioners with reference to the maintenance in their hospital of destitute persons suffering from in- fectious disease. Mr. Marshall, secretary to the Com- missioners, states in the letter that the Guardians refused to pay an account of £B3, and says the Health Commit- tee desire their lordships to institute an inquiry into the matter so that it may be definitely settled which Autho- rity was to.bear the costs. In reply to this request, Mr. Adrian, assistant secretary to the Local Government Board, replied that it would be competent to the Run- corn Improvement Commissioners to refuse to admit pauper patients into their hospital except under an ar- rangement with the Guardians providing for the cost of the maintenance, &c., of-the patient in hospital, but if no such arrangements had been entered into and pauper patients had nevertheless been received it did not seem to the Board that any liability was attached to the Guardians. The Board suggested, as regarded the future, that an arrangement should be entered into forthwith embodying such an arrangement.—Mr. Tay- ler said he understood that the Guardians had an agreement with the Commissioners with regard to pauper cases.—The Clerk said that was the fact but it was for cases sent to the hospital by the order of the Guardians or where there had been an order of justices sending the pauper to the Hospital. In none of the cases taken into the hospital had that been done.—Mr. Taylor said a number of the cases were ordered by the relieving officer.—The Clerk : I think not, and even if Mr. Cooke did give an order, he had no right to do so according to the agreement.—Mr. Taylor : The Guardians only meet once a fortnight, and we must give the relieving officer a certain amount of latitude. Mr. Norman said they were in honour bound to pay for the destitute cases whether there was an or- der er not.—Mr. Aaron said in opposition to that the Local Government Board said they must not pay. There- fore it was a waste of time discussing it.—Mr. Cooke was called into the .room and said he never gave an order for a person to be taken to the hospital; nor had he asked the sanitary inspector to take them into the hospital. In several cases he had given a medical order and the persons mere taken to the hospital, but not by his order. There was not one in the whole number in, receipt of out door relief. They were rnostly men on the road searching for work on the Ship Canal.—Mr. Norman thought they were in honour bound to pay for the eases with a .medical order, without grumbling about the exact terms of the agreement being carried out.—Mt. Aaron said .Dr. MacDougall was medical officer to the Commissioners as well as to the Guardians, and he had no power to give a patient any order, and the Local Government .Board said they must not pay.— The Chairman : That is how I read it.—Mr. Taylor asked would not the Commissioners have power to send these pauper cases 2—The,Clerk : No. You cannot send infectious diseases to Dutton. The Sanitary Authority must deal with them.—Mr. TaN lor held that the Com- missioners had honourably carried out their parts of the agreement, and the Guardians had passed under similar conditions during the past .five years. Tie was not now Waking for the Board of Commissioners, but he dared say that the question would now be how could the Guardians be forced toadeal with these cases. He thought the Guardians had taken a high-handed stand.—The Chairmaq Said he did not see how the Commissioners, under the agreement, Could refuse to take them into the hospital, when they were sent. —Mr. Taylor said there had never been a case sent by the Guardians to the hospital.—The Clerk said he should not advise payment of any item in the accounts rendered wi :bout the sanc- tion of the Local Government Board.—Mr. Norman gave notice that at the next meeting he would move that the resolution declining to pay the accounts be rescinded, and that payment be made in those cases where it medi- I 'cal order had been given.

ACKNOMILEDOMMTS FROM 31 P.'S. Letters were read from Mr. Henry Tollemache M.P. and Mr Alan de Tattoo Egerton acknowledging receipt of suggested amendments to the Parish Councils Bill and stating they should receive attention. TRAMPS.

The Bridgeworth Union forwarded a reso/ution draw- ing the attention of the Local Government Board to the alarming increase of tramps throughout the country, and suggesting that uniform rules as regarded bedding dietary and task work should be enforced.—TheCarmock Union submitted the following resolutirm fir approval That the Casual Poor Act of 1882 having tailed to have.any effect in suppressing vagrancy this Board think that the time has arrived when the Local Government Board might provide for uniformity of treatment on the lines of the way ticket system.—The subject was re- ferred to tke Parliamentary Committee. COUNTY RATE BASIS. The Northwich Union forwarded for the approval of the Runcorn Union, a resolution protesting against the present inequitable county rate basis, which had not been altered since 1886, notwithstanding great thanges in the rateable value of townships. They again asked the County Council to take measures for the adoption of a new basis and to urge the Committee to give due con- sideration to section six of the County Rates Act 1852. They were of opinion that the standards for the county rate should be based on the gross estimated rental, ac- cording to the valuation list in force for each township for it such method were adopted the standard made would not only be an equitable one, but its revision at frequent intervals would be greatly facilitated, and the complaints from all parts of the county as to the present inequalities which had been so numerous for the last two or three years, would entirely cease.—The Clerk said there were some inequalities in Runcorn Union. In 1886, the rateable value of Overton was £12,403, present time, £14,582 ; Dutton, £10,532, at present, £10,040 ; Frodsham, £11,477, at present, £13,167 ; Elelsby, £5,696. at present, £7,576 ; Runcorn, £51,933, at present, £58,674; Weston, £10,633, at pre- sent,£l2,l33.—Mr: Woodward said the places where the rateable value had been reduced were paying an unjust propprtion of the rates.—Mr. Handley said it was neces- sary that they should have the county rate on a proper basis, and he moved that a similar resolution to that of the Ncrthwich Unimlto the County Council.—Mr. Wood- ward seconded . Carried unanimously.

FRIDAY.

In the House of Commons on -Friday evening Mr. Mun- della, answering a question, said the quantity of foreign coal imported into this country during August;Septem- ber, October, and November, was 15,641 tons. Replying to a question about a recent sentence passed by the Recorder of Liverpool (Mr. Hopwood), Mr. ASQUITH said he had no jurisdiction to interfere where the only complaint against a sentence was that it was too lenient. The law did not need strengthening, and its enforcement must rest in the discretion of those whose duty it was t administer it. Mr. Buxton, interrogated by Mr. Labouchere, stated the present position of the negotiations with Lobengula, and said he was confident that Sir H. Loch would neglect no opportunity of corning to tl-rms with him, so as to bring about as speedily as possible a peaceful settlement in Matabeleland.

The House again went into Committee on the Local Government (Parish Councils) Bill. Progress was reported at eleven o'clock in order that the report of the resolution in Committee authorising the Secretary for India to raise in the United Kingdom a aura not exceed- ing £10,000,000 for the services of the Government of India might be taken. Mr. G. RUSSELL (Under Secre- tary for India) explained the abnormal monetary condi- tions which had led the Indian Government to consider it prudent to obtain sanction to this loan. After some criticism from Sir J. Gorst and Mr. Goschen, the report was agreed to, and a bill founded on the resolution was brought in and read a first time.

In the House of Lords, the Earl of DUDLEY moved an amendment to clause 4of the Employers' Liability Bill, to the effect that it should not apply to any agreement between employers and men for insurance against ie- jury providing the agreement was sanctioned by a majority of workmen and gave reasonable compensation. The Earl of Denbigh preferred a majirity of two-thirds. In the course of the debate Lord Ripon and Lord Chan- cellor opposed contracting out, the latter believing that security to life and limb would be diminished. The Bishop of Durham spoke in its favour. Lord Salisbury said personally he should be glad to see the insurance of working men made universal, but suppesing the bill passed without the Earl of Dudley's amendment what would be the result ? Employers would protect them- selves by insurance against all risks. On a division the Earl of Dudley's amendment was carried by 148 votes to a, and the Earl of Denbigh's proposal for a two-third majority was accepted. the bill passed through Com- mittee.

===== 0002601_18931216_art0027.txt | included=true | included =====
SATURDAY.

In the House of Commons on Saturday consideration of the Local Government Bill was resumed, discussion being continued on Mr. Cobb's amendment, which sets forth that in case of every parochial charity, not being an ecclesiastical charity, the parish council shall, not- withstanding that the number of trustees may have been fixed by the instrument creating the charity, appoint additional trustees from the ratepayers, electors, or inhabitants to make a majority of the whole body. After much debate Mr. Balfour moved to report progress, and an animated discussion followed, the Opposition arging that the sitting was beyond endurance, and the Liberal members that the prolongation of business was due to obstruction. Mr. Balfour's motion was rejected. Mr.Asgorta said the Government attached great impor- tance to the disposal of Mr. Cobb's amendment, and they were willing that Mr. Banbury's amendment should be added to it, to the effect that no person not qualified for election as a parish councillor should be I appointed a trustee, and that no person should continue te be a trustee after he had ceased to be so qualified. The Committee divided on the amendment as amended, and there were—for it, 109; against, 48 ; majority for, 61. The announcement was received with Liberal cheers.

MONDAY.

- - In the House of Commons on Monday evening Mr. ASQUITH, in reply to Sir F. Dixon-Hartland, said the question of what arrangements ought to be made to pro- tect the House from outrage was one for the authorities of the House in conjunction with the police, and both were satisfied that every necessary arrangement was being made. Sir W. Hancounr followed with a statement in which he announced that it was intended to despatch, en be- half of the House, a message expressive of sympathy with the French Chamber with reference to the outrage of Saturday, a course in which Mr. Balfour, on behalf of the Opposition, expressed hearty concurrence. Sir J. LUBBOCK moved the adjournment of the HMse in order to raise a discussion on the refusal of the Government to agree to the proposal of the House of Lords for the appointment of a Joint Committee of the taro Houses to settle the principle of "betterment." Sir W. Harcourt defended the decision of the Government. Mr. Chamberlain said the House of Lords had conceded the principle, and the only question was as to the best method of applying it, and he thought their lordships should have a chance of retrieving their error. In the course of the debate reference was mado to the new street improvement bill of the Manchester Corporation and Mr. Pickers„eill, said a telegram had just been re- ceived from the Town Clerk, of Manchester which said, Our betterment clause is to test the principle of bet- terntent. The area is not large, and the estimates are not yet completed." Mr. Balfour accused the Govern- ment of wanting to pick a quarrel with the House of Lords. The motion for the adjournment of the House was defeated by 17750 139. The House then went into Committee again en the Local •Government (Parish Councils) Bill. After a long discussion and a threat on the part of Mr. Balfour that unless the Government" treated the Opposition with un- flinching honesty of purpose" the Bill would become absolutely impose ible, clause 13 was passed by 143 to '9O. TUESDAY. In the House of Commons on Tuesday evening, Lord GEORGE HAMILTON gave notice of his intention to move a resolution calling upon Her Majesty's Government to make before the Christmas recess a statement of their intentions in regard to the maintenance of the efficiency of the navy. Mr. ASQUITH informed Mr. J. Ellis that he had re- ceived from the Miners' Federation of Great Britain a resolution urging the Government to resist the insertion in the Employers' Liability Bill of any clause permitting contracting out. Mr. Burnie, in replying to a question, read a telegram from Sir H. Loch which stated that the Matabele ap- peared to be returning in small parties. Mr. 3. K. HARDIE moved the adjournment of the Muse in order to call attention to the destitution pre- vailing in various parts of the country, and to indicate the means by which, in his opinion, the Government might alleviate it. Sir. W. FORSTER disputed Mr. liar- die's figures, said the Government were doing what they could in the direction of the organisation of industry, pointed to the efforts being made to improve the condi- tion of the people by legislation, and expressed the opinion that for the relief of distress they must rely on the action of local authorities rather than on that of the Central Government. Mr. Batrone joined in the dis- cussion, and, referring to trade disputes, said he theught it was forgotten how intimately the prosperity of the working classes was bound up with the profitable invest- ment of capital in this country. The motion for ad- journment Was defeated by 175 to 33. The House then went into Committee again on the Local Government (Parish Councils) Bill.

In the notice cf Lords, on the motion of the Earl of KIMBERLEY, a message of sympathy with the French Legislature and the President of the Republic on the occasion of the recent outrage in the Chamber of De- puties was agreed to. The Marquis of SALISBURY, In expressing approval of the course proposed by the nohle Earl, said he hoped it might be taken as a pledge that if any international steps were taken to cope with these wicked outrages the Government of this country would take their share in achieving so humane a result. In reply to a question as:to what steps the Govern- ment intended to take to mitigate the distress in London, Lord MONIESWELL said that all they could do was to watch the course of events and make such representa- tions to the local authorities as in their opinion would mitigate the evil. WEDNESDAY. \

In the House of Commons on Wednesday Mr. George Russell moved the second reading of the East India Loan (£10,000,000) 13111. The discussson was continued by Sir IL Temple and Sir John Gorst. Sir JOHN Lunnocx expressed apprehension at the policy of the Indian Government, and said they were really engaged in speculation in sil- ver. Sir W. HARCOURT said what the Indian Govern- ment had asked for was a present remedy for an immin- ent evil. They did not deny that the closing of the mints was an experiment of a serious character, but the difficulty- was a serious one, and Ministers were not prepared to adopt the bimetallic remedy. There had been a temporary disturbance of trade and exchange by the closing of the mints, but that was inevitable. The imports into India had, however, greatly increased, and that increase was due to Manchester trade. Manchester had always been crying out for 'remedies touching the monetary system, and DOW she had got what she wanted the result had been an Increase of trade ; still that had operated against the exchange. He spoke the opinion of the Indian Government. They thought the disturb- ance that had taken place was temporary, and that it could be dealt with by a temporary remedy. Mr. GOSCHEN maintained that the remedy had caused a dis- location of commerce. At the risk of displeasing his Lancashire friends, he felt bound to say that it was not the business of the Government to hold up an artificial rate of exchange. After further discussion the closure was carried and the bill read a second time. The House adjourned: at forty minutes past five o'clock.
