HELSBY WATER SCXEME.

The minutes of the Belsby Parochial Committee showed that at a meeting held on Saturday the 9th inst., a letter was read from the Cheshire Lines Committee, stating that their annual consumption of water at flelsby would be 4,000,000 gallons, and if a water scheme were carried out they would be pleased to take their supply from it, instead of from the present uncertain source, always assuming that the price arranged would be reasonable. The writer (Mr. Meldrum) however, does not commend the committee to any guarantee.—After consideration the Parochial Committee resolved that it be pointed out to Mr. Meldrum that thY supplying of 4,000,000 gallons of water per annum in addition t 4 the ordinary supply to the township would make a consid- erable difference as to the sort of scheme to be carried out, and that he be requested to state whether if a scheme sufficient to give this extra supply be carried out, his committee would undertake to take at least 4,000,000 gallons of water per annum at the price of 6d. per 1,000 gallons.—The minutes were approved.

THE SHIP CANAL. Mr. Wells, the engineer to the Board on the Ship Canal, said he was on the canal from Twenty Step Bridge to Runcorn, on the 27th November, after the flood had subsided and with the exception of some damage to the old sluice and masonry at Keckwick brook he had nothing to report concerning its effects. With regard to the syphon at the same spot, the pipe had been laid through the subs ay, hut the sewage was not taken through them as yet. The new door connected with Reckwick brook was not fixed, and a portion of the existing masonry was destroyed by the recent flood, which burst from the canal at this place and nearer Runcorn, and overspread the lowlying land along the margin of the brook. As the masonry and new doors would be above the ordinary level of the surface of the,. water in the canal, the reinstatement could be effected independent of the height of the water.

THE MEDICAL OFFICER. Dr. Adams writing from Brighton. said he had been laid up with severe influenza followed by bronchitis,and would therefore be unable to attend the meeting. There had been only a few fresh cases of infectious diseases notified, and these he thought, were all in connection with cases previously reported. THE CLOSING OF THE SCHOOLS. The Rev. R. K. Nunn, chairman of Norley School Managers, said a meeting had been held relative to the closing of the schools and the managers submitted to the Authority the following facts :—That the offices had been recently cleansed and disinfected; that no bad smells had been detected ; that it could be proved that the sickness did not originate from the school; that most careful precautions had been taken to revent the spread of infection through the school, that the fever had virtu- ally disappeared ; and that the throwing of nearly 100 children out of school work and into the lanes for five weeks at the present season of the year was calculated to induce more sickness. It was hoped that under all the circumstances the Authority would withdraw the closing notice.—The Medical Officer of Health and the Sanitary Inspector were instructed to inspect the sani- tary, arrangements of the school now that alterations had been made.

WARRINGTON BOARD OF GUARDIANS. The fortnightly meeting of the Warrington Board of Guardians,was held on Wednesday, at the Work- house. There were present :—Messrs. G. Ratcliffe (in the chair) R. Ileaton, J. Dignuro, S. Davies, J. Hill, A. Jolley, A. J. Jolley, M. Mather, J. Muir, J. Turton, J. Houghton, W. HoughtonV. Dobson, and Dr.J. N. ratterson, also Dieser& J. C. Sutton (clerk) S. Sutton (assistant clerk), C. H. Ward (master) and 11. N. Mines (Superintendext Yadgate Schools.)

AN EXPENSIVE MAT. Attention was called in the passing of the accounts, to the purchase of a mat intended for the office of Mr. Aukland. the relieving officer, the cost of which was EI 163. 6.1. Several members thought that the price was exorbitant and that an inferior mat ought to have been ordered.—Mr. A. J. JOLLEY objected to articles being informally ordered by a member in his individual capacity.—Mr. HEATON said the Board ought to be called upon for their consent to such a purchase ; other- wise an officer might order an elephant skin at a cost of £2O or £3O. (Laughter.)—Mr. AUK LAND was called into the room and said that the mat was intended for his office on School Brow. The purchase was sanctioned by the Board in July. Mr. Pilling had seen the mat but he did not go with him to buy it. —Mr. hILL: It is too much, 163. 6.1. would have been a more reasonable price than £1 163. 6.l.—Mr. Aukland : It is a cocoanut mat, nine feet in length, a yard wide, and very thick.—Mr. Shaw thought the price was a reason- able one.—The Chairman intimated to Mr. Aukland that the purchase did not meet with the approval of the Board and the subject then dropped. FIRE ESC&PE SHOOTS.. _ _ .

The Workhouse Master's report notified that the fire escape shoots which had been ordered from Messrs. Ross and Co., had arrived.—The CHAI RIMAN : Are they suitable.—Mr. WARD : They arc very short. In fact I should not like to come down one.—The Board decided to inspect the shoots.

AN IiktPERTINENT LETTER.

The Schools Committee's report contained a recom- mendation that the new stoves for the school rooms be ordered from Messrs. Musgrave and Co.. Belfast.—Mr. Shaw asked if the Committee in passing the recommen- dation had considered the letter of a Ic.scal tradesman which had been sent in.—Mr. A. JOLLEY : I should like to know, to. I think consideration ought to be given to the application of a tradesman of the town. (Hear, hear.)—Mr. A. J. Jolley said the letter had not been considered and Ivs hoped the Committee would never consent to consider such an impertinent letter as that was. (Hear, hear.)—The CLERK explained that the recommendation was decided upon weeks before the letter arrived. It had been decided to have Musgrave's stoves and to obtain them from the manufacturers, as they could supply them at the cheapest price.—Mr. lIHAToN :Of course. It would be another .injustice to Ireland if we didn't. (Laughter).

COAL TENDERS. The following tenders for the supply of coal for the half-year were submitted :—Alfred Perris for house coal, 12s. 9d. per ton; slack, Bs. 2d. per ton ; coal for Pad- gate Schools. 133. 4d. per ton. The supply to be regu- lated by the strike clause. Messrs. Fletcher and Bur- rows, Atherton: For house coal, 12s. Bd. ; slack, Be. For coal for Pedgate Schools, 13s. 4d. The strike clause also to be recognized. From Messrs. Richard Evans and Co: For house coal, 11g. per ton ; slack, 7s. 7d. per ton. For coal for Padgato Schools lle. Bd. The strike clause was not mentioned in this tender. It was decided to accept Messrs. Evans and Co.'s tender and several members ex- pressed their satisfaction with the manner in which the Company had treated the Board during the coal strike. This was ll the business of a public character.

SUICIDE OF MIL GLADSTONE'S VALET.

An inquest was held on Thursday on Z. Outram,lately a servant in Mr. Gladstone's employment, and whose body was found in the Thames on Tuesday. No cause could be assigned for the suicide, if suicide it was, and the jury returned an open verdict. Mr. Herbert Glad- stone said the case was a very mysterious one.

The Brighton Liberal Association met on Monday night, ana d-cided not to contest the seat vacated by Sir William Marriott. Mr. Bruce Wentworth, the Con- servative candidate, will be returned unopposed.

HOW A LOT OF MONEY LEAKS OUT.

WILAT does a man do when he finds a hole in the pocket where he carries his money ? Anybody can answer that question. He has it sewed up, of course, directly, and good and strong*too. I suppose it is with you just as it is with me. When I spend money, even foolishly, I can tell where it went, and maybe I've had some sort of pleasure out of it. But I do mortally hate to lose money ; lose it out and out, you know, and have no satisfaction from it, or know how or when it left my possession. Well, now, let me show you the worst and biggest hole any man ever had in his pocket ; a hole that lets the cash leek away like water through a sieve, a hole that is the hardest in the world to sew up. A short story will show it best. " Drummore South Cottage, " Musselburgh, near Edinburgh, " September 18th, 1891. " Gentlemen,—Up to 1885 I was always strong and healthy. About this time I began to feel bad. I was tired, languid, dull, and listless, and everything was a burden to me. Iliad no desire for company, and what had come over me I could not make out. My tongue and mouth were dry, and I had a deal of phlegm on my stomach. The whites of my eyes next became discol- oured and my skin was yellow. I had no appetite, and after sating I had great pain at my chest and sides, also across my stomach. After a time the pain settled in my left side, and my heart would beat and jump in a manner that alarmed me. By-and-by I got so weak that I was not able to go ab3ut the house, and I felt that I ought to be in bed. The pains at my side and stomach be- came so bad that I had to remove my clothing (every- thing seemed so tight), and I used to press my stomach and hold my sides to try and ease the pain. Getting worse I saw a doctor at Musselburgh and was under him for three months, but his medicine gave me no relief. After this I went to a clever doctor at Pres- tm Pans who said I was suffering from indigestion and dyspepsia. He sent to London for some celebrated medicine which was packed in small phials. This medi- cine seemed to dissolve my food, and I felt easier for a time but I gained no strength or real benefit, and after . persevering with this treatment for six months, I gave it up and fell into my old state. I next went to a doc- tor at Musselburgh, but all his medicines did me no good. After this I saw another doctor (that is a fourth. doctor), but with the same result ; none of them gave me anything that reached my complaint. I now lost all. faith in physic, for I had OW a great deal of money and taken so much medicine' that I lost all my teeth through it' and was no better for it. In great misery I lingered on month aftr.r month, always ailing, when in, August of last year (18901, my husband called at Mr. Jack's Drug Stores, nigh-street, IFisherrow,: and told him what my condition was. Mr. Jack gave him an account of the wonderful cures he heard of from many of his customers that had taken a medicine called Seieel's Syrup, and strongly recommended him to bring me a bottle. lie did so, and I commenced taking the Syrup, and I found some relief Crom the first bottle, and by the time I had taken four bottles I was as well and strong as ever I was in my life, and have since k• pt in good health. I tell evervone what Seigel's Syrup has dune for me, I never thought to get better again,' and I consider it has saved my life. I wish others to know this, and if by publishing this statelnent it will be the means of helping others, Its it helped me, you can use this letter as you like. Yount truly, (Signed) Jemitna Watson.

Look back to about the middle of the:above letterand again read what tho writer says : "I had spent a deal of money for medicine." Yes, and money she could.poorlv afford to spare. Illness and the expense of illness is the great hole in the pocket that I alluded to. It costs so mach, and what does it give us in return ? Pain, weariness and misery. There is another consideration besides. When we are ill we not only have to bear the increased outgo, but manage to meet larger demands out of a decreased income. Our candle is burning at both ends. "Yes," you say, " but how can we keep from falling ill ?" You cannot always, but in view of the fact that most illnesses arises from indigestion and dyspepsia a timely use of Mother Seigel's Curative Syrup will pre- vent it. A few shillings thus invested will save pounds in money and perhaps months of wretchedness. Think over tho striking points in Mrs. Watson's excellent letter and you will think the same.

• the debate on Home Rule, whether the majority or the minority shall rule. Mr. GrAnsroNE pointed out that while the Local Government Bill of 1888, which was promoted by the Conservatives and consisted of 129 clauses, passed through Com- mittee after twenty-two nights discussion, the present Bill, which contains only seventy-one Clauses, has already been debated on twenty nights and is yst far from the end. The PREMIER then in his quietest and blandest tone asked, " Is it unreasoaable to go forward with this Bill," and he was immediately ans wered from the Tory benches with loud cries of " Yes." We have not the slightest doubt that those who shouted "yes "—although Mr. BALFOUR afterwards attempted to throw them ever—represent the real feeling of the Opposition. In spite of all Protestations to the contrary, the Tories are des- Peratelv anxious to withhold the right of self- government from the rural districts. In order to effect their end, it is now as clear as daylight that they are prepared to utilise all the devices of ob- struction. But, fortunately, their mischievous Policy has been clearly unmasked both to the Government and the country, and it is evident from the language of Mr. GLADSTONE and his col- leagues that they are ready to moot the tactics of their opponents with such vigour and determina- tion as will ensure the passing through the •House 'f Commons of the Parish Councils Bill.

Trades Unionists Tun London correspondent of , and the " Manchester Guardian " ranployers' bilit -Fstates that it is now rumoured that several of the Peers are beginning to "wobble" in their hostility to the Employers Lia bility Bill. The House of Lords have inserted the contracting ent clause in the Bill, but this, of course, does not end the matter. It is practically certain that the Government will not accept the amendment of the Upper House, and, therefore, their Lordships will have another opportunity of reconsidering heir policy. Groat efforts have been put forth to Make it appear that the House of Lords was really acting in the interests of a large body of work- Illen• Deputations of a mtg.° or less bogus char- acter have waited upon LORD SALISBURY and other Tory leaders to urge the scivantages of contracting out. But it is now abundantly clear, if there had ever been the slightest doubt, that the great mass of the workingmen of the country will be satisfied With nothing less than the Bill as it left the °use of Commons. The Trades Unionists have Lune after time asked for such a measure, and Tiow that it has been conceded to them by the re- Presentative Chamber they are not going to allow liernselves to be robbed of its moat vital benefits the body of irresponsible landowners and pluto- crats who control the Tiotio of Lords. The Par- liamentary Committee of the Trades Union Con- have decided to organise demonstrations in favour of an unmutilated Bill, and there can be little doubt that their appeal will be responded to In every considerable town in the kingdom. Trades Unionists are put upon their mettle in this mat- ter. They have, so to speak, the ball at their feet. All that is required of them is that they 41.1euld make tioemselves heard. Especially is this the ease in a town like Warrington, which has the inisfortnne to be represented by a member who has both spoken and voted against giving addi- tional protection to workmen. We are convinced that Mr. ROBRICW Frnaporxr is not voicing * sentiments of the working population of the town in his opposition to the Liability Bill, and if that he so, the organised trades of the town should let him and the country know it.

Imp THE public generally, and especi- ortant An Conference. ally the residents in the colliery districts, cannot fail to be deeply interested in the joint conference of mine-owners ~."(1. miners which met in London on Wednesday. "le conference is one of the outcomes of the Inetnorable meeting under the ch airmanship of 14°r, d RoannEny which Bottled the g reat dispute. Inat settlement, it will be remembered, .was more 6f the nature of a trace than of a permanent Pace. The great point of getting the men back tel.work was secured, but several important ore mat- ,., 8 w left over to be dealt with at the Confer- 'lce between masters and men, the first meeting of tWhich was held on Wednesday. That an ami- cable arrangement should be come to is in the kg,he3t degree desirable. Another cessation of saikaucur in the Federation districts— whether in the of a strike or a lock-out—is, we may take 't.ufor granted, not to be thought of. The mere ggestion of sech a possibility would raise uch a feeling in the country that neither side Would be able to resist the pressure that would b 0 brought to bear against it. The meeting the fourteen delegates on each side on ednesday ards afternoon was a rather prolonged :al lasting about four hours, and it was after- that the delegates had not been h eto agree upon a chairman. This contingency kad, however, been previously provided for, and t "a Speaker of the House of Commons will now be :tBkad to appoint a chairman. On the whole, it :Perhaps better that the chairman should not be n° rn i nee of either side. Under any circum- stances he will have a difficult office to fill, and being aPPointed by an outside and impartial authority he will be less open to any imputations nilo‘" lavonritism.

OUR GR&TIS ALMANACK.

To each purchaser of this issue of the " Examiner " we present our Annual Sheet Al- manack.' It will be found to contain several features of an interesting and useful character. It is embellished with a striking and characteristic view of the Viaduct which conveys over the Ship Canal, the London and North-Western Railway at Latch- ford, taken at a time when the process of excavat- ing the cutting was yet proceeding. The engrav- ing is a reproduction of a photograph by our res- pected fellow townsman, Mr. T. Dirties, of Sankey- street, who has done so much good work of this character. As time dims our recollection of the actual work of construction of the great waterway, the illustration will become a valuable souvenir of the many formidable difficulties, which were en- countered, and overcome. In this respect, the choice of subject may be claimed an appropriate one. There is the Viaduct, forming a fair average instance of the enormous expense which has beer. incurred in the erection of bridges, and the diversion of railway routes. There is the strip of unfinished channel, which is one of the best illustrations that could be afforded of the strata of rock which was encountered, with such dishearten- ing frequency, in the work of excavation. Then again, the view is associated with two of the most important features connected with the whole un- dertaking. They are, the Latchford Locks, and the Hydraulic Pressure House,which supplies the motive power to four of the ponderous swing bridges that have been erected,as well as to the locks themselves. The locks may be seen in the distance beyond the viaduct, and the hydraulic station on the left bank of the Canal. Thee two important pieces of work, together with the viaduct, have alone swallowed up, in their construction, close upon a million of money. The viaduct itself was formally opened for traffic on Sunday the 9th of July, when the first goods train to cross it was the 8.15 a.m. to Liver- pool. It is about 70 feet high, and is described as a 1,220 ton bridge being the second heaviest Viaduct that has been erected over the canal. The locks and the hydraulic pressure house were put into working operation on Thursday the 7th of December, when the first vessel, the "Snowdrop" passed along the waterway from Eastham to Manchester. In addition to the illus- tration, however, there is given in the Sheet Alman- ack a vast amount of valuable local information, which, without being guilty of egotism, we may fairly claim to be more comprehensive than that contained in any other similar local publication. It embraces officially revised matter appertaining to all our various municipal, parochial, ecclesiasti;al, military and civil institutions, as well as to local political Clubs and Associations and elections. Much trouble has been taken in elaborating and compiling this mass of facts and figures, and there has been introduced into the calendar a consider- able amount of valuable memoranda of notable local and general events.

The prisoner, who was an old offender, was committed ' to gaol for 14 days' with hard labour. FARM FIRE NEAR WIDNES. On Tuesday night, shortly before seven o'clock. a fire VMS discovered in a barn of Samuel Whitfield', Crow Wood Farm, near Widnes. In the barn was stored about twenty tons of wheat straw, and a number of farm im- plements. The flames spread to the shippon and stables, and the three out-houses were all ablaze before the ar- rival of the fire brigade. The fire was got under about nine o'clock, but the barn, shippon and stables were completely gutted. The damage is estimated at £lOO, and is covered by icsuiance. FATAL FALL DOWNSTAIRS AT Ito:scour% On Tuesday, Mr. Worsley, coroner for the fee of Haltou,held an inquest at Runcorn, on the body of Anne Parks, aged 40 years, of Mersey-street. The evidence showed that the deceased lived at the lodging-house of Tbos. Murphy, and on Saturday night she had some drink. At midnight the deceased and Murphy were sit' inc besides the fire, when she got up and a few minutes later Murphy having occasion to go into the yard, found her lying at the bottom of some Fteps bleeding from the left ear. Dr. Robinson was called in, but he was unable to do anything for her and she ex- pired on Monday morning without having regained con- eciousness. Her neck was broken. A verdict of " Accidental Death was returned.

THE ALLEGED MANSLAUGHTER OYA CHILD AT RUNCORN.

At Runcorn Police Court, on Wednesday, before Mr. Philip Speakman, John Thomas Corker, and Susannah Corker man and wife, were charged that they did feloniously and unlawfully kill and slay their infant son John Thomas Corker, aged ten months; Mr. Giles, of Cnester, prosecuted on be- half of the Natienal Society for the Prevention of Cruelty to Children. James Uphill, inspestor to that society, deposed that on the 29th Novemher, he visited the house in Vicar-street, where the prisoners lodged, and saw two children of the prisoners named Sarah Ellen and Mary Elizabeth aged respectively six and four years. Both were poorly clad, were very filthy, their bodies bearing numerous marks of vermin bites. The smell Crum them was very offensive.. In a bedroom he found the deceased lying on a filthy mattress and the mother tried to hide him by throwing her apron over him. The deceased wore a nightdress only, and his body Was in a dreadfully dirty condition and made the room stink. The child's body was greatly emaciated, his legs were drawn up, and there were two bed sores on the right hip. There was no food in the house for the child except a quantity of sour milk. Next day he found the same conditions prevailing and be had the child photographed by Mr. Picker. The photo was put in and showed the child to no in a shocking state. On Saturday the child died.—Florence Gorst, of High-st., and Laura Kirkham, domestic servant to Miss Hughes, High-st., gave evidence as to the children of the prison- ers begging, the eldest carrying the deceased —Thos. Cooke relieving officer, also spoke to the mother and children begging. At the beginning of November, the mother applied for a medical order for the child and lie gave her into. Neither the mother nor child were clean, they stunk the office jut. Ile thought the child was then in a dying stato.—His Worship retired to consult with the clerk (Mr. Ashton) and the deputy clerk (Mr. Johnson), and out retiring into Court, Dr. McDougall, who had given evidence last week that death had been caused by lung disease was recalled. In reply to Mr. Ashton, the doctor said the death of the child would be accelerated by unnecessary exposure,and want of clean- liness and a want of general personal attention.—The prisoners were then cautioned in the usual manner, and the male prisoner said they hot done all they coulc for the child. He had bought what nourishment he could affeird. He had got milk, isinglass arid port wino and lie had fed the child before feeding himself, when it had shown a desire to eat. He had put bread dipped in gravy in its mouth and it had thrown it aside. He had also bought pork pies for it and it would not eat them. The mother said the child had also had the breast, and she had One all she could. The child got so thin that she was almost afraid to wash it.—The prisoners were , committed for trial.

GILCHRIST LECTURbS AT RUNCORN.

IThe last of the series of Gilchrist lectures was delivered in Runcorn Public Hall on Thursday ' evening, by Professor Seeley. F.R.S., of King's College, Oxford, who dealt on the subject of Volcanoes. There was again a crowded audience. The Rev, Canon Maitland Wood, rho presided in the absence of the Rev. Dr. Preston through illness, referred to the great interest that had been die- played in the lectures, and of the large amount of information that had been imparted by them, and then asked what was to be the result ? lie hoped the lecture would he a means of stirring up increased interest in the good woz k which they trusted would soon be accomplishea in that town, namely, the bull ling of a scientific and technical institute. The estimatc-a had already been sent in and accepted. fie was also to add from Dr. Preston that if they 113,1 a museum started in the institution it would be a great , pleasure to him to present his specimens to it, and he had an excellent collection. (Applause.) He trusted that other lectures would be given in that town, and the attempts would not meet with the cold water they had experienced in the past. They had a good deal si anxiety about alcourse of Oxford Extension Lectures, and be was sorry to say that the committee was still indebted to the Oxford University to the sum of about £35. 'Plat led up to the fact that at the beginning of the new year they were going to have a lecture delivered early in the new year by the Secretary of the Oxford University Committee, Mr. Sadler. If they were favoured with another course of lectures from the Gil- christ trustees, they would feel doubly indebted to them, and there were hopes,that in a few years the lecturers would be willing to come and see 'Runcorn again. (Ap- plause. The Lecturer remarked that the term "volcano" was ' understood to mean more or less the conical elevations upon the earth's surface which had been built up by material ejected from below. When they considered the vast number of enormous elevations scattered over the earth's surface, and especially near the shores of great oceans, they would imagine that there must be some method in those disturbances if nature by which the frequently molten material from the interior was poureii upon the surface. The force which produced volcanoes was in some way related to the force which heaped up the mountain chains, upon which volcanoes were fre- quently reared, and was also closely related to the force which heaped up islands and great masses of land. Extinct volcanoes were to be found in the interior of continents, but those which were active were always to be found near the sea shore. fhe question arose, "What force in nature was capable of heaving up such large portions of the surface of the globe?" The farce. was heat, and then the question presented itself, "Whence came the heat ? " This he could not tell with absolute certainty, but he could tell them what was the theory of advanced scientists. They believed that the heat had been in the earth from the beginning, and that it was part of the great plan by which the earth was brotieht in- to existence. It was due to the force of gravitation which di ew towards the centre of the earth those materials of which the earth consisted. Owing to time resistance which those materials offered to the various processes of me- chanical energy it became converted into heat. As the earth shrunk more and more towards its interior the temperature of heat inevitably became higher. It was generally supposed that the interior of the earth was 1, molton, but it was beyond all question solid. There was I the phenomena ot the tides due to the action of the sun and moon, which represented the difference between the heaving of the water and the heaving of the land. The globe had been proved to be rigid as much so as a globe of rigid steel. The reason why the high internal heat did not liquefy the globe was that liquefaction could only ensue when it was capable of overcoming the pres- sure by which the substance was surrounded, The answer to the question as to what caused volcanoes was that they were the results of the escape or loss of the internal heat of the earth. The lecture was beautifully illustrated by numerous photographs shown by the oxy- i hydrogen lantern. The Chairman proposed a vote of thanks to Professor I Dee!ey and also to the Gilchrkt Trustees for permitting I the town to have the very great advantage of these lec- tures. They had sent admirable men who had interested them very much. The vote was carried by acclamation and Professor Deeley, in reply, said the Gilchrist Trus- tees would learn with pleasure of the prok.ct of a Techni- cal Institute,vvhich he had no doubt would be the means of bringing happiness to many.

EXPLOSION AT THE SEAMEN'S MISSION AT RUNCORN. On Sunday morning, about 11-30, the convegation worshipping in the Institute connected with the Mersey Mission to :ieamen, Runcorn, were startled by an explo- sion ; but anything like a panic was prevented by the officiating clergyman,the Rev. C. F. Montgomery.recom- mending his hearers to remain seated. On examination, it was discovered that one of the pipes connected with the heating appAratus, in the room below, had burst, forcing away the top of the stove, and throwing the burniog material allover the room. The red hot cinders set fire to the block floor, and the place was filled with smoke. Prompt efforts resulted in the extinguishing of what might in a few minutes have proved a very serious fire. Fortunately no persons were in Use room where the explosion occurred at the time. PaucEAßD's have now stood the test for fifty years, and quality and first class workmanship are OAR guaranteed dirt.

- - POSTAL ARRANGEMENTS FOR CHRISTMAS. Owing to the fact that this year, Christmas comes on a Monday, it is rather difficult to make proper arrangements for the increased traffic which invariably occurs at that time. The post office authorities had issued notices requesting the public to post their letters and parcels not later than Fri- day, and they are anxious that the public should tall in with this suggestion. As every one knows the train service on Sunday is very different from that on ordinary week days, and there are not the same mail trains on that day as other days. There- fore lithe posting be deferred until Sunday, it is vary probable that many Christmas good wishes will not be delivered until Tuesday morning, when Christmas is over. Furthermore parcel work is not done on Sunday. At the same time, the authorities are making extensive arrangements to cope with the traffic, and the staff will be strengthened by having special carriers. The staff will also be sup- plied with coffee, &c., and everything will be done to make them comfortable. There is one favour which the post office authorities are particularly anxious to ask of the public, viz., that they will not offer drink to the postmen at Christmas time. 'FTPLENDID NEW CIJIIRA.NTS 2d. per lb. at COLIIER'S.—Adrt. CLAIM FOR A WATCH. —INTRRESTING TO PAWN- ruioxsens. At the Warrington County Court, on Thursday, before his nOTIOUT Judge Wynne Ffoulkes, Dan Coyne, Kerfoot-st., sought to recover a silver lever watch which had been pledged with the defen- dant Mr. E. V. Morton, pawnbroker, Winwick-st. Mr. Wyse, solicitor, Manchester, represented the plaintiff. and Mr. Unsworth, solicitor, Warrington, defended. From the evidence of the plaintiff, it ap- peared that in March, a man named Heron, son of a traveller, in the employ of Mr. Wyse, watch maker, of Manchester, who had sold a watch to him,called upon Coyne, and told him that the watch had got out of repair, and he promised to take it to the maker, Mr. Wyse to have the repairs executed. Plaintiff had never seen the watch since.—Mr.Wyse, of Manchester, who had instituted•the proceedings, said that the watch was supplied to Coyne to be paid for in weekly instalments. The instalments not having been kept up, he called upon Coyne. who told him that he had given the watch to Heron to take to Manchester to have it repaired. Witness eventually discovered that the watch had been pawned by Heron at the defendant's shop. Hs th.n obtained Coyne's consent to commence pror—,,ilngs against defendant for the recovery of the watch.— Mr. Unsworth submitted that although he admitted that by common law, the plaintiff was entitled to the watch, no demand had been made by the plain- tiff for it prior to the action being instituted, and that, therefore, the claim could not be made out. • Mr. Morton had received the watch in good faith, • not knowing that it had been stolen.—His Honour, ! however, held that the service of the summons was sufficient demand, and gave judgment for plaintiff.

THINGS HEARD AND BEEN AT ItUNCOItN. BY AN OCCASIONAL CORRESPONDENT.

Emboldened by the successor the nrize distribu- tion, and concert last winter, Mr. .1. F. Moulton,the head teacher at Greenway-toad Board School, has engaged the Public Hall, on Monday evening next, for a similar demonstration. The progress of these schools since their erection has been very rapid and the SUCCOSs achieved complete. Handicapped at, at the commencement with unpromising material in the shape of " outcasts" from other schools, and with very few other scholars, the school is now tilled to over-flowing in every department and the results achieved at the Government examinations are in every respect highly satisfactory. In fact, such a character has the school attained that par- ents are continually applying for the admission of their children, but, unfortunately, in consequence of the lack of room, education has to be sought in other institutions. Tee School Board have just taken a step which will obviate to some extent this drawback, by deciding to extend the girls' school, so as to provide accommodation for about 80 more scholars, hut judging from appearances the time is not far distant when a greater and more complete extension of the school will have to be made. The teachers deserve every credit for the high standard of efficiency to which they have brought the school. Punctual and regular attendance is of the utmost importance,if the pupil is to thoroughly master the subjects taught, and at (31reenway-road Board schools these two things, punctuality and regularity.are attained by a series of magic lantern entertainments, which are instructive as well as amusing, and by the giving of prizes to those de- serving of reward, and they are many. The neces- sary funds, it may be necessary to state, do not come from the rates, but are raised in a manner which is applicable to every other elementary school. The ratepayers have every reason to be proud of their schools. Last year Mrs. Brunner dis- tributed the prizes; on Monday evening, Mrs. Tom- kinson, of Withington Hall, will perform the cere- mony,snd also give an address. As Mrs. Tomkinson is a bright and polished speaker, and a sincere educationist , her observations, will no doubt he well worth listening to.. The entertainmenz promises to be interesting andl diverting. --- Northwich Union is to he congratulated on initia- ting the movement for obtaining a revision Qf the basis of the county rate. A revision has not been made since 1886, and in the long interval that. has intervened,there has nu doubt keen a general rais- ing of the rateable value, while in individual town- thins, great changes have taken place. The com- munication read at Runcorn Board of Guardians on Tuesday, showed that at liwtock Gralam in North wich Union. the, rateable value had nearly doubled, and in many others there were striking increases. The decreases were small and unimpor- tant. Mr. Ashton. the clerk, presented some figures which showed a total increase of .£15,000 in the rateable value of five townships since 1886. It was unanimously decided to support the ,Appenl of Northwicla Union for a revision of the basis, and there is every relson to believe that the County Couneil will yield to the request.

The proposal to memnralise the Queen to grant a borough bench of magistrates met with little favour at Widnes Town Council on Tuesday. Only the proposer and seconder Councillors O'Donnell and Gough. voted in favour of the proposal, an unmis- takeable evidence that the Corporation are satisfied with the present magisterial arrangements.

THE NHW.BUILDING BYELAISS.

At the Town ll all, on Thursday -tri-o-;;;ing-;: an in- formal meeting of Runcorn Commissioners was held for the purpose of considering the report of Mr. Barker, the surveyor, with regard to the ohjec- tions of the builders. Lake (law clerk), E. Mar- shall (secretary), and M. Barker (surveyor). After discussing the subject in private for about an hour and a half, it was decnied to admit the Press.—Mr. Humphreys, Chairman of the Byelaws Committee, said the committee were prepared maintain their position, and he hoped the Board would stick to their decision to adopt the byelaws.—ln reply to Mr. Prit- chard,the surveyor said the clauses in the byelaws with regard to closets all pointed to the advantage of the water carriage of excrete and dry pits for ashes. SCANDALOUS CRITELTY TO A CHILD AT

WARRINGTON.

TILE BOARD OF GUARDIANS CENSURED. Alice Atkins, 309, Winwick-road, was brought before the Warrington magistrates yesterday, on the charge of cruelly iliteeating Mary Ellen Connor, her adopted daughter, on various dates between September 15h, and December 7th. Mr. E. S. Ciiles, solicitor, Chester, who prosecuted on behalf of the N.S.P.C.C., said that the cruelty COM- plAined of was of a serious and atroci.ms character. The defendant some time age, went to the 'Board of Guardians, and asked permission to adopt a child as she had none of her own, After some time, the child, Mary Ellen Connor, who was eight years of age, was chosen. She had since treated the girl very badly, ono of the complaints elle made being that the girl did not get up early enough to light the fire. However, that might be, the defendant had struck the girl on the body repeatedly with a double leather strap. She had also illtreated her with her fists, and •n one occasion she had damaged her hand with a knife. Doctor Bowden exam- ined the girl on Saturday and found both eyes blackened and there WAS a scar of scratching on t he right cheek,the scar of a wend on the right ear, and right arm, besides extensive bruises on various parts of the body, and an incised wound on the little and ring fingers. The defen- dant had tried to explain the wounds on the fingers by saying that she took a knife out cf her hand. The defen- dant's conduct had been such that they would scarcely expect an animal to be guilty of. The little girl who was almost a baby was told to brush up, get the break- fast ready and other things before altos wont to scheol, and it appeared that the defendant could not control her temper and this was the result, because the girl Was unable to do all that was expected of her. Inspector Uphill, N.S P.C.C., said that the child i was brought to hie shelter on the Bth inst. She had then two black eyes. Ile went on to describe the various inim ries which he and Dr. Bowden discovered on examining the child's body. The child was well nourished. Ile went to defendant's house soon after the examination of the girl and asked her how she could account for the inju- ries. She replied that she could not account for them at all, unless they were done at school. She said that the girl was insured in the British Workmen's Club. Witness aftervrards laid information, and a warrant was issued. The child had been in the shelter ever since. Dr. Bow ten described the injuries, and bore out Mr. Giles' St tiement. He considered that they were caused by a blunt instrument, and a strap could cause them, or being knocked against the wall. Some of the blows on the legs had caused thickening of the bone, which indi- cated a very severe blow. Mary Ellen Connor, the little girl in quesrion,said that she was taken out of the Workhouse by Mrs. Atkins to live with her. She wanted her to serve at the table, sweep up the cruinhe,and wash up the cups and saucers before she went to schen!. She was only eight years of age. Mrs. Atkins hal hit her across the eves with a strap becsitie she lied not made the fire, and also hit her across the fine..rs. She had sometimes to go to school withont breakfse. Maria Knorr, who called for her on the way to school, gave evidence to seeing Mee. Atkins heat the girl once. Harriet Barrett, a neizlibour, and Elizabeth Wnolla- cstt, schoolmistress ef Orford schools, also deposed to seeing the girl's eyes black. The latter said that the girl was atient from school about twice a week, but she was H. very good and clever child. She was never beaten at ;he school. Mr. It Shearer, inquiry officer for the Guardians, said that the case was a most exceptional one as it was the first instance in his recollection, that the Guardians had ever allowed a child to he adopted. The woman made an eppliration to Vie Workhouse matron and asked to be allowed to have a rhild,no matter how young,to have as a daughter, as she had none of her own. The matter came before the House Committee,snd he (Mr. Shearer) was directed to visit defendant's house to see if there was any accommodation for the child. He made every inquiry, and they appeared to be respectable people,and seemed to he very comfortable, and the house wae very clean. Upon his report, the child, who was one of three orphans, WAS allowed to go under their charge. The responsibility of the Guardians ceased after this tran- sac'inn. The husband was a teetotaller at that Vine. Mrs. Atkins upon being sworn, denied any illtrest- ment of the child. She did not know how the black eyes were caused nor how she had so many injuries. Mr. Atkina,Mrs. Bibby,and Mrs. Lightfoot, each cave evidence to the effect that no ilitreatment of the child ha I been committed. Alderman Harrison In riving the decision of the Bench said that a penalty of £2 and costs would be inflicted. He had been a member of the Board of Guardians for 27 years, and during the whole time he was a member of that Board such a case its this could not possibly have taken place. He could not help thinking that due care had not been exercised In allowing the child to be adopted by persons who had only been three years in the town. He thought that a very great amount of blame snd responsibility rested upon the liaard of Guardians for what had take rt p!ale. A more atrocious case had never come before the beneli. THE CANAL A r RUNCORN.

Dredging operations are being extensively carried on in the Runcorn district, and there appears every reasoa to believe that the requisite depth will be reached in the course of a week or two. The dam at the old Quay has been removed. On Monday 'at noon an exciting incident occurred. A mud hopper,had been filled by the dredger Barry, and was being towed town the canal by the steamer Liberator. When near Runcorn Bridge the hopper " listed" over throwing the huge buckets of mud into the water and also the crew of two men. Ose man could swim whilst the other had not that accom- plishment, but both was saved by the crew of the steamer after strenuous exertions. Both men were in an exhausted condition. The buckets were recovered on Wednesday. The shipping trade at Runcorn has eon- aiderablv improved during the ptst fortnight, many vessels, coming both by the Nleraey and along the Ship Canal, having entered the docks. On Saturday after- noon the Katandiu arrived in the layby at Runcorn, con- taining 1,857 loads of deals, consign. d to Messrs. Pierce Watts, and Co. She is the largett boat to come alnng the new waterway so far as Runcorn. She draws 201 t 6in. She entered the canal at Eastharn on Friday after- noon at two o'clock, and after reaehing Ellesmere Port she grounded on the slope. Here she had to remain until the midnight tide when she floated, put it was considered advisable to wait until daylight before proceeding further along the channel. The steamer African Prince,764 tons burden, arrived in Salt- port on Friday.and left on Monday with a general cargo for Mediterranean ports for Messrs. Gotieh and Cross- thwaite, of Liverpool. A fie, named Ann Maria, belong- ing to Mr. Abel, general carrier, Runcorn, sailed up ta Warrincton with material far the Ship Canal, arid another', known as the Red Tail, belonging to the same owner, was the first to pass through the Widnes' lock ,the gates belonging to which worked in a satisfactny manner. His flat Snowdofl. also came from Weston Point to Runcorn, on Friday, in full sail. SOME INTERESTING PAR TICULARS. The Warrington Corporation, we understand have at length arrived at a settlement with the Ship Canal Com- pany on the matters which have been in negotiation for ;tome time past. The Ship Canal Company have un- dertaken to complete the dredging of the river within six months after the opening of the canal for traffic. ['Mae manufacturers who are now using barges and boats in the river are to have the use ,f the canal And the right of using the Rencorn and Lateliford canal, passing into it at Twenty Step Bridge. free of toll until the dredging is completed, and all other manufacturers and traders of Warrington will have the right to use the canal on the it Janus ryfrons Eastharn to hatch ford.and alto the right to use the lock atTwentyS,er Bridge andthe canal leading to the river on preciriely the same terms as those contained in the Act of 1885 for the bentlt of the traders of Warrington, namely 41, per ton. This privilege is secured to the traders until the dam and the lock at Walton are completed and made available for traffic. To-day (Saturday), a visit to the canal will be made by representatives of the press, at the invitation of the directors of the Company. For the information of the party an excellent handbook has been published by the Company. containing some very interesting memoranda. According to this useful publication it enema that the area of land that has been purthased is 4.520 acres, and the amount claimed for purchase money aed riompenm- don £2,195,519. The two high level road bridges across the canal at Latch ford and at Warburton, known as the Cantilever bridges, have spans .1 306 leet and a clear head way of 75 feet from water level Rid] bridge weighs 783 tons, and has a road way 25 feet wide. There are seven high level railway bridges, ranging in spans from 137 feet to 266 feet. The heaviest bridge is at Acton Grange. Its weight being 1,916 tons, whilst the next heaviest bridge is at Latchford, weight of which is 1,220 tons. Thomas Brimelow, butcher, Winwick-road, was brought before the Warrington magistrates on Monday, charged with furiously driving a horse and trap in Liver- pool-road on December 4th. Mr. NV. Knowles defended. Frederick Skelland said that on the 4th inst. he was going down Bank Quay bridge at about 7-45 p.m. in company with his brother-in-law, Henry Ficton, when they heard a horse and trap coming down the bridge at great speed, and when they got opposite Cresfield's lodge the wheel come off. The horse continued its course with the trap which bumped along for some distance.—Henry Pictcn corroborated the last witness. Mr. Bunt : " How fast do you consider the horse was going."—Witness :"Thirty miles an hour "(great laughter). Mr. Monks (presiding magistrate) : " Don't you mean thirteen." Witness : "No, thirty." Alderman Harrison : " Why that is rail- Nap speed. In answer to further questions, witness said that he was well acquainted with horses and fre- quently went out with them —Jacob Humphries also gave corroborative evidence.—Mr. Knowles submitted that if the wheel had not come off, the case would never have been brought into the court. He denied that there was any furious driving, and said that the wheel came off owing to a faulty axle.—Wm. Brookes said that he was in the trap on Bank Quay bridge at the time the accident occurred. Be did rut consider that it was being driven too fast.—Wm. Parkinson, and Joseph Massey also gave corroborative evidence and the magistrates dismissed the case. CRUELTY TO HORSES. WOMEN'S TEMPERANCE ASSOCIATION.

BRITISH

MEETING OF THE WARRINGTON BRANCH.

A meeting under the auspices of the Warrington I branch of the British Women's Temperance Asso- ciation, was held in the Gospel Temperance Mission Hall, on Monday night. There was a good attend- ance, and the chair was occupied by Mrs. H. Roberts. There were also on the platform, Mies Green (railway Mission), Miss Bessie Dutton, Misses Bradbury, Miss N. Roberts, Miss Mary Gornall, After an opening hymn, and prayer by Miss Green, Mrs. BOBERIS, in her opening remarks, said that that was the first time she had found herself in the position of occupying the chair at a public meeting so that she must ask them to excuse her if she made any mistakes. In looking around the room, she thought that they had great cause to congratulate themselves upon the pro- gress they were making. It was encouraging to meet together to strengthen each other in the good work in which they were engaged. She very much enjoyed the meeting at the Co-operative Hall a month ago, of the Social Questions Union. The two addresses of Mr. Arm- strong and Mr. Aked were full of interest. One thing that impressed her was the remark of Mr. a.ked tlat al- though the temperance party was making progress and gaining ground in the country, drunkenness was on the , increase amongst women. She thought this was a pain- ful thing, and one which the British Temperance women must take into consideiation, and they ought to do everything to overcome it. (Hear, hear.) Each mem- ber of that society ought to find and influence one of their erring sisters by their earnest sympathy, and help them to overcome the temptation. (Hear, hear.) She still remembered a saying of their dear old friend, Mr. • McMinnies, who used to say that a good chairman should be seen but very little heardsand she would profit by that saying, and go on with the programme. (Laughter and applause.) MISS GREEN moved the following resolution which was to be sent to the Premier, the Chancellor of the Ex- chequor, and the Member for the Borough :—" That this meeting, while acknowledging with gratitude the value of the recognition of the important question of the liquor traffic legislation in the Queen's speech, and heartily thanking the Government for the introduction of the Local Control Bill, in February last, expresses its great disappointment that the Government felt them- selves compelled to defer it, and calls upon them to in- troduce the Bill on the earliest date in the session of 1894," She said that she had great pleasure in moving the resolution. It was time for everyone to do battle against that 4readftil thing, strong drink. She felt that it was the strong drink that had taken very much of the g-Zory from old England, and also a great deal of the sunshine from their homes. They had heard judges say that 90 per cent, of the cases which were brought before them had resulted from strong drink. They ought to influence public opinion and the magistrates on this question. She considered that one public-house to 1,000 people to be a large proportion, but in Warrington there were about 250 public-Louses, which was a very large proportion indeed. It was, therefore, absolutely neces- sary that the public-houses should not be multiplied,and that the magistrates should not have the power to mul- tiply them. k Hear, hear.) , Miss BESSIE BUTTON seconded the resolution. Se said that at their last meeting at the Co-oporative Hall, the resolution was passed, and had been sent to Mr. Fierpoint, and she had received the following reply : " Dear Madam,—l beg to acknowledge with thanks, your letter of yesterday on the subject referred to in it, and shall give due attention in the event of the Bill being revived." He evidently had some doubt of the Bill be- ing revived. (Laughter.) She hoped that he would take the right course when it was again brought into the House of Commons. The Bill was a good one as far as it went, but they thought that the two thirds majority required was too great, and that the simple majority was sufficient. The drink traffic had wealth on its side which it would use for its own purposes, and the other ,1 site would not have this advantage. She therefore I thought that a simple majority would ba sufficient. The Bill also did not go far enough. Hotels and clubs would not be influenced by the Bill. A great deal could be done , by the women of the country, by bringing pressure to , bear upon the minds of the legislators. They had sent , a representative to the great meeting in London on the , previous Wedrissday, and everyone who had attended ; that meeting spoke of its enormous importance. She herself had been present at Rhyl on Friday at a meeting of the Flintshire delegates of the British Women's Society, at which Lady Henry Somerset was present,and the meeting was most enthusiastic. She was convinced that it was necessary for Sunday school teachers to be- come total abstainers. The ytsung people around them were not only looking to their words, but also to their lives, and the teacher should not influence them for any- thing but good, and shauld refrain from anything that would cause them to go wrong. During the meeting Miss Mary Gornall gave two " Intermezzo " solos upon the violin in an excellent man- ' ner,and Miss N Roberts sang "Come to me," and "Glory , to Thee,my God this night," for which she received loud ' applause. The usual votes of thanks concluded the meet- ing.

THE EXAMINER, SATURDAY, DECEMBER 16, 1893.

FOOTBALL.

Secretaries of Football Clubs are requested to note that reports of matches played on Saturday must be sent to the head office, Bewsey-street. Warrington, not later than ten o'clock on Wednesday morning.

GEORGE G. BUSSEY and Co. are leading makers of Footballs, Hockey, Golf, and requisites for Sports. Games, and Gymnasia. Catalogue may be had on application to the Sports Manufactory, Peckham, London, S.E. BEST < Cep (t.

RUGBY. WARRINGTON v. SWINTON.

Fortune did not shower her bounteous smiles on War- rington on Saturday, when the club met its strongest opponent, Swinton. Nevertheless, she did not shower any more smiles on Swinton. Both clubs seem to have secured an equal share of the Fickle Jade's favours, for the game was a draw. It was a draw in two senses, however, for in addition to the fact of equal scores mak- ing the game a draw, the game itself made a large draw of spectators. No less than 700 persons accompanied the Warrington team to Swinton, and in addition a tremend- ous " gate" of Swintonians was present. It was a great source of gratification both to Warrington spectators and players, that the club should have so successfully competed with the toughest of their antagonists. Indeed, it has been whispered—but utter it not to Gath—that if Warrington can only maintain their form of the past few weeks there is a great chance of their gaining posses- sion of the coveted cup. May the whispered prophecy find fulfilment. Both teams were representative ones, and were as follows : Swinton : W. Winterbottom, back; F. P. Jones, J. Valentine, W. Pearson, and J. T. Lewis, three-quarter backs; H. Brockbank, and R. Flosker, half-backs ; H. Case, G. H. Murray, T. Rothwell, F. Knowles,W. Evans, T. Hallam, A . Sharpies, and G. Sharpies, forwards. War- rington : F. Heesom, back; E. Potts, P.Carey,J.W.Evans, and J. T. Massey, threequarter-backs ; John T. Bate, and James Bate, halt-backs; Fair. Turner, J. Jolley,J. Berry, T. Donohue, W. Nevins, J. Taylor, J. Buckley, and E. Bailey, forwards. Fair Turner set the ball into motion, and things very soon assumed a merry character. Very shortly after, James Bate made his mark, and then Jolley twice fol- lowed witli splendid forward play. But the Swinton forwards eventually managed to get the oval among them and charged down upon the Warrington line with very dangerouslooks ; but Heesom was sale and soon sent the invaders back to their own territory. However, like the proverbial bad pennies, the Swinton men "turned up again"—near the Warrington line. At one time they came very near scoring, but, were again frustrated by James Bate introducing one of his characteristic runs, and taking the ball with him. The centre flag was once more reached, but just at this point John Bate unfortun- ately received an injury which caused him to leave the field. His place was filled by Evans, and Nevins was 'drafted to three-quarter. Upon the resumption of play the Warrington three-quarters made themselves remark. ably conspicuous by the clever manner in which they followed up a pass by James Bate, a performance which was terminated y a splendid kick from Nevins. Play was thus taken into Swinton territory again, and, although the home backs made the most com:nendable attempts to return play, they could not claim success as theirs. However, in the course of a desperate struggle for mastery, Pearson picked up the oval and with a grand ovrintinvaded Warringtou quarters,and,although it was predicted by the home people that he would score, Heesom proved how uncertain is prophecy by interven- ing between Pearson and the goal line. Scrimmages followed, after which there set in a period of fine give and take play. A splendid attack in which James Bate, Potts, Evans and Jolley had a hand, carried hostilities into the Swinton " 25," where, from a scrimmage, James Bate passed to Potts, and the latter struggled thrmgh and scored a try. Turner failed to augument. From the drop out Bailey made, his mark and again Turner tried to place a goal without success. Then Ta lor kicked, and the ball going in the direction of Valentine that artist nipped up the leather and cleverly handed it to Pearson. The latter putting on top speed dashed through his opponents and wound up by gaining a superb try. Valentine failed to place a goal. After this both teams made strong efforts to score, and the visitors, now playing their fall complement, had decid- edly the best of the game. The half-backs were playing very determinedly, and the tackling on both sides is described as being not exactly of a drawing-room char- acter. At half-time the score was a try each. In the second portion similar tactics to those obtaining in the initial thirty-five were seen, good exchanges between the backs being followed by Pearson interceping a pass between the Bates, but Pearson Showed a want of pluck unlike his usual form, and after speeding away about twenty yards he seemed quite content to be tackled. Heesom fielded the ball several times very smartly, but did not kick with much forge. The game was now in the Warrington half, and a fine rush, in which flosker, Case and Valentine were prominent, gained further ground. Carey relieved, and then Jones got the ball from Brockbank, but was too well tackled to become dangerous. Play became very lively at this stage, and the ball did not remain in one place very long. Wen- . . . .

tine put in some useful punts, and the vcsitorsiline was threatened after a dribble by Murray.. Taylor was prominent, and Carey dribbled to the Swinton 25,where Winterbottom smartly relieved. Another rush by War- rington almost ended in Massey scoring, but, after a I scramble, relief was afforded by a fine rush, and further proceedings were waged in a more neutral quarter until no-side was signalled, when the score was :—One try (three points) each. WARRINGTON Av. SWINTON A.—After an absence of 1 five weeks from the football field, the Warringtonians encountered a strong team of Swintonians on the Wit- derspool enclosure, before a goodly crowd of onlookers. The home team started operations against the wind, and for some time the game was located in neutrll territory. Then a free kick against the visitors was splendidly utilized by Freeth, Fligham, Hampson, and Coggins dribbling to the line, and from the ensuing pack Mason passed out to Raybold, who scored far out. F:or lengthy period the play was fast and furious, but no- thing of a substantial character was forthcoming,whilst free kicks were nun3erous,Swinton eventually attacking, Stevens having a golden opportunity of dropping a goal, but the kicker was too eager, the ball going wide of the posts. Some splendid dashing work by the Warrington forwards, in which Thornton, Freeth, Eligham,flampson were noticeable, forced the visitors to defend, only to see a ietaliatory movement by the Swinton forwards carry the ball to half way, Boscow saving the rush. Lawless, jun., who was playing in splendid fashion,was again prominent, and then Thornton broke away with therball at his toes. A fine kick by Mason saw the visi- ting custodian's kick charged, but Berry smartly re- covered possession, and Bowker relieved the danger. More free kicks followed, Swinton being subjected to a heavy pressure,until the visitors broke away to Boscow, who replied to touch at centre. Rayt,old, Edleston, Edwards, and Bandies were then conspicuous, Okell replying with a long punt, which rolled dead. Harris soon afterwards got away finely, enabling his side to press the homesters rather severely for a time, when Edwards and Smith transferred the

warfare to neutral territory, half time arriving. Upon the resumption Swinton attacked vigorously, Coggins ultimately making a tine dashing run past half way. Lawless, jun., was soon conspicuous, but Swinton broke away in fine style, until Boscow checked their onward career. A splendid bout of passing between Lawless, sen., Mason, Edlesten, and Lawless, Jun., took the con- test to the the Swinton line, where Smith scored. Play after this was very fast, but there was no more scoring. Warrington were thus winners by two tries to nil.

WARRINGTON ST. MARY.S V. WIDNES RANGERS.— Played at Warrington on Saturday. The homesters were short of Burton, Welsby, and Berry, but the reserve men very ably filled the vacancies. The visitors started the ball, the game at once becoming of a very even nature, until Widnes, by very neat passing, scored, which re- sulted in a goal. After this the Saints rallied, and took the ball to the Widnes line, where Woods and Warburton had hard lines for scoring, Warburton once grounding the ball, only to be ruled back. Nothing more being scored up to half-time the score stood—Widnes,one goal; St. Mary's, nil. On changing ends the homesters con- tinually pressed. but could not score, and the game began to look as though it would end in a win for the visitors, until Swift picked up neatly and dashed over, Hill converting. On dropping out the visitors were again forced to defend,and had to touch down on several occa- sions.but managed to bring the game to a close—a draw, each having scored one goal.

LATERFORD RANGERS V. WARRINGTON HORNETS.—This match was played on the Latchford ground, on Satur- day. The visitors, who were much the heavier lot, kicked off. Play was of an evenly balanced character during the first half, but no scoring was done. After the interval the home team kicked off, and at once carried play into the visitors' quarters. Mather got hold, and passing to Johnson this player was grassed before he could get far by Ashton. A dribble by Read and Old- royd took play back on the visitors' lines, where Bram- hall had hard lines in not scoring, the ball rolling dead. The drop-out being charged down found matters looking bad for the visitors, until Johnson cleared with a kick to the 25 flag. The home forwards not to be denied rushed the ball hack to the line, where a scrimmage was formed, , and Warburton getting hold scored the first try for I Latchford, After some vigorous play Breadon got hold, and passed to Newton, who made a good run, but was pushed into touch a yard from the line. From the line out the home forwards dribbled over, Dennett scoring. The latter end of the game was of an interesting char- acter, but no additional scores were notched.

WinNks v. WALIMEN.—The above clubs met on Satur- day, on the Widnes ground, before about 2,000 specta- tors. The commencement was delayed 45 minutes owing to the late arrival of the visitors, who only brought 13 players with them. Two substitutes were obtained on the field to cmnplete the complement. Walk- den commenced hostilities, and the home men at once assumed the aggressive, and rushed down into their opponent's quarters. When play had been in progress about ten minutes Clark get possession and landed safely over the line, but Barber failed to improve. The Widnesians still pressed vigorously, and Clark almost scored again, but was floored. Drummond then got the oval, and gave a long pass to Barber, who scored splendidly, but the kick at goal was fruitless. The Widnesians gave the enemy no chance, and kept the ball near the home line, where two or three scrimmages took place. Toohey picked up, and got home behind the goal, and Barber easily converted, A few minutes afterwards Drummond made a determind rush, but was brought down Toohey, however, got possession again, and placed the oval safely home once more. Barber neatly landed over the bar. The visitors still had not a look in, and two minutes before half time Drummond got over and Barber was successful at goal. At half time the score stood—Widnes 3 goals, 2 tries; Walkden, nil. When play was resumed the ball could scarcely be sean on account of the darkness, and after play had con- tinued for ten minutes the referee blew the whistle and ended the game. The result was Widnes. 3 goals, 2 tries ; Walkden, nil. Widnes was represented as follow, : —Back, Brennan: three-quarterbacks, Atforde, Toohey, and W. NEW BRIGHTON V. RIINCORN.—There was a good atten- dance at [Award on Saturday to witness the first of the season's engagements between these Cheshire teams. The Ituncormans started, and, following the usual exchanges, play settled down in the centre. Abrams eventually got the ball away, but Warder was at fault. Cannel' dribbled on, and the Runcorn half was invaded. Faulkner checked the movement, but Drinkwater inter- cepted a-pass and made headway, following which Bleasdale sent ahead, enabling Cannel to cross the line. Ellis failed at the place. Following this reverse, the Runcnrnians got away strongly, and a couple of free kicks were awarded, but nothing tangible came of them. For some time the play located in close proximity to the Brighton line, and here Ellis put in some capital saving work, and later led on a strong rush to the centre. Houghton again transferred the play to the opposing end, but the tackling of the Brightonians was clean and safe. Eventually Faulkner passed out to Abrams, who romped over the line in good position, but Warder failed , to convert. Following the drop out, the play ran on fairly even lines, but ultimately the ball was again ' worked to the home line by Butterworth and Richardson. After packs had been formed near theoline, Richardson again got possession, and ran in near the corner, But- torworth failing at goal. This brought about the inter- val, when Runcorn were leading by two tries to one. On resuming the general play ran favourably to the Runcornians, for whom Richardsm scored his second

try. No other points being scored up to the close of the game, the final result was ;—Runcorn, 3 tries ; New Brighton 1 try. Teams ; New Brighton—Simpson, back ; thinkwater, Lewis, and Cannel', three-quarter backs •, Bleasdale and F. Lura, half-backs ; Williams, Ellis, Miller, Walker, Clare, Spragg, Saividge, Smith, and Gullan, forwards. Runcorn—Houghton, back ; Richardson, Butterworth, Morris, and J. Warder, three- quarter backs; Abrams and J. Faulkner, half backs ; Reece, Langley, Davies, Farmer, Moores, Williams, Tay- lor, and Jordan, forwards. The "Liverpool Mercury" thus comments on the game, "The visits of Runcorn are always productive of a stir, even in the lethargic circles of local Rugby foot- ball. Their game on Saturday was with the New Brigh- ton team, and the result brought about the first defeat of the seasiders this season. There was a capital muster of spectators. The opening stages of play were of a sensational character, for before the game was a minute old Drinkwater intercepted a pass, whereupon Cannell joined issue, and to the consternation of the Runcorn section of the crowd a try was gained barely five minutes from the start. This was an eye opener for the Run- cornians, but they speedily recovered, and in turn Abrams and Richardson ran in tries, but neither was converted, and Runcorn led at half time by two tries to one. The after portion of the game was mainly in favour of the wearers of the green,for whom Richardson scored his second try, and New Brighton were beaten by nine points to three. Both sides were well balanced,but in paint of cleverness the Runcornians were quite two tries ahead of their opponents." ST. HELENS RECREATION V. TUEBROOK.—At St. Helens, before about 1,000 spectators. Tuebrook started, and Molyneux returned to touch. WARRINGTON ST. EEPRIN'S V. ORFORD BARRACKS.— Played at Dalton Bank qi fine weather. The Saints were without the Rev. T. W. Cook and Forbes, their places being taken by Phcenix and Sherwood. Scarisbrick kicked off, the home front rank making tracks for the Barracks goal, but Jones was handy and neatly robbed. iaylor and transferred to midfield, Tickell getting pos- session and, neatly evading Reid, placed in the goal mouth, Taylor scoring. From the re-start Tweed's and Turner got well down, Mirshall intercepting a grand shot from Turner. For a few minutes play was in mid- field, and then Radford got possession and passed to Kickinbottom, who shot high over the bar when he had the goal at his mercy. Tickell followed this up with a fine throw in the goal mouth,Hatton heading the secoad point for the Saints. This was quickly followed by a third from Marshall. The Barracks were now showing I up, but could make little headway against Colley and Marshall. Half-time :—St. Elphin's, 3 aoals • Orford Barracks, nil. Jones re-started, and the Barracks made a good attack, hut thanks to some capital work by Sher- wood and Marshall, Jones's charge remained intact. A grand bit of work by the Saints front rank was well met by the Barracks defence. Hatton, when in a good position, missed two easy chances, and then Turner had a shie at Jones, who cleared in fine style. Phcenix gave to Scarisbrick, who shot wide, Taylor doing likewise a moment later. A foul against Phcenix in the goal mouth enabled the Barracks to score. Final result :- 1 St. Elphin's, 3 goals ; Orford Barracks, 1 goal.

WARRINGTON L. AND N. W. ALBIONS V. WARRINGTON POST OFFICE.--The above match was played on the ground of the former, Arpley Meadows. The visiting team arrived late and two thirties were agreed upon. The Albion won the toss and Post Office kicked off. Give and take play occurred for about ten minutes, when the Albions, through a combined movement by the whole of the front rank, drew first blood with a good shot from the foot of Evans. Eventually the Post office broke away. but the Albions' defence was impregnable, and from a return by Griffiths, Connor fi..r the Albions scored. Half time arrived with the score— Albions, 2, P.O , nil. Restarting the P.O. played up but it was of no avail, the Albions defence being perfect. The railway men ran the leather down, and Connor added number 3. The Post Office now played up for all , they were worth, and a splendid shot by Dykes hit the crossbar, going dead. They were at last driven back, and the Albions put on another point, and the whistle blew with the Albions pressing strenuously. Final result Warrington Albions, 4 goals, Warrington P.O. nil.

WARRINGTON ASSOCIATION V. CHESHIRE MAGPIES.—The Magpies came to Warrington with an unbroken record, expecting to add another win to their list. The hsme team were without Mather and Linaker. Connorkicked off. For the first few minutes the Magpies pressed, till Clarke relieved. Connor went off with a grand run, but his shot went wide. The Magpies again took up the running, and forced a corner from Forrest. Immedi- ately after they scored from a free kick near goal. They again attacked, and sent the ball through, just as the whistle went for hands. Warrington now had a turn, and a good shot by Reid almost scored. Fennelley raced down the left and scored with a grand shot from the corner. The home team still pressed, and from a judi- cious pass by Reid, Connor scored. 'Norman had a grand chance, but shot over. The Magpies pressed, but could do nothing against Clarke and Evans. Half-time : Warrington, two goals ; Cheshire Magpies, one goal.. The Magpies tried hard to force the game, but the halves and backs kept steady and prevented them getting too near. Phillips ran through and passed to Connor, who transferred to Norman, the result being a neatly shot goal. Warrington again attacked, and would have scored but for a splendid save by the Cheshire goal- keeper, who cleared his lines while on the ground and and surrounded by the opposing forwards. Clarke sent back a rush of the Magpies, and Fennelley getting hold ran well and forced a corner,which he placed beautifully enabling Connor to score. Final result : Warrington, five goals ; Magpies, one goal. PENKETH A.F.C. v. ASTON HALL.—This return match was brought off at Aston Hall in beautiful weather. The home team started the ball, and five minutes later Hornbill opened the score for the visitors. This aroused the visitors, and before half time arrived seven more goals were put through by them. Restarting, Pen- keth had the game all their own way and put on four more goals, and an interesting game ended in a win for Penketh by 12 goals to one. PRNRETII RESERVE V. In the first half the Lymm forwards played badly but improved considerably in the latter portion of the game. At back Wilson, Holt, and Dodson were in grand form. WARRINGTON GRAMMAR SCHOOL VERSOS WIGAN Y.M. C.A.—This match was played at Warrington on Wed- nesday in windy weather and resulted in a win for the Grammar School by 2 goals to 1. HOCKEY.

GRAPPENHALL V. BOWDON (A).—Played on the ground of the latter. After about 15 minutes' play Gibbon scored the first point for Bowdon from a good centre by Moffat. Soon after Owen equalised for Grappenhall. Up to this point the play had been very loose, but after some good passing Gibbon scored the second goal for Bpwdon. Just before half time Bowdon secured a corner, wilich was fruitless. After a few minutes' play Moffat secured the ball in his own 25, and Tan almost the whole length of the field to the corner flag, and from a splen- did centre Gibbon was able to score his third goal. Picken shot the fourth and last goal from a bully in the circle. Owen, Burroughs, and Carter played best for Grappenhall. Final result : Four goals tx) one in favour of Bowdon.

CLERGY DAUGHTERS SCHOOLS. PRESENTATION OF PRIZES. The annual distribution of prizes at the Clergy ' Daughters Schools took place yesterday week in the lecture room of the institution. There was a crowded audience, and the chair was occupied by Mr. Charles J. Bushell, the president. The proceed- ings opened with two trios, " The Robin," and "The Dove," by senior students. The annual report was then given by the Rev. Morley Stevenson who said that he hag a very good report to make this year. They were very much pleased with the result of the Government examination, as of their senior students, one half were placed in the first class, and not one was in the third. Even better results had been ob- tained in the religious knowledg examination, because of their senior students. 42 were placed in the first class, and the reniaining nine in the second class. The colleg-e at the last examination stood first in the whole king(l2no in domestic economy, which was a. subject in which girls always should stand high in. Most of those present were aware that the college had acquired the schools which adjoined, which really formed part of the college. They had to raise a large fund for the purpose of acquiring them, in fact £3,000, but the whole of this had been raised with the exception of £2OO. Among other donations, they had received £5OO from the late Lord Winmarleigh, and £5OO from Lord Lilford, £3OO from the Duke of Westminster, £lOO from the Board of Education, £lOO from the National Society,and an equally generous donation from Mrs. Beamont. The students, past and present, had also raised £lOO. (Ap- plause.) Next year would be the jubilee of the college, and on the 23rd June, it was intended to hold a sale of work, or some other means of raising the money neces- sary to wipe off the debt. This year wa, the tenth of his (the principal's) work at the institution, and he had spent a happy time amongst the students. He spoke in high terms of his co-workers for the help they had given to him during that time. The Chairman next made a speech in which he spoke of the great influence upon the religious education of the coming generation, which was exercised by the day school teachers. After the Christmas Carol, " There was silence in Bethlehem's field,"(Stainer)had beemsung by the students the distribution of prizes took place. They consisted of books,the donors of which were the Rector of Warrington (Rev. F. W. Willis), Rev. W. H. Binney,Rev. M. Steven- son, Rev. E. J. Willcocks, Rev. T. L. H. Milla, Mrs. Stevenson, Mrs. Beamont, Messrs. P. Pears, C. White, S. E. Haworth, and W. S. Woods, and the committee of the institution. The presentation was made by the Pre- sident, mid there were 23 successful prize winners among the first year students. and 18 second year students. At the closeof the meeting " Dulce Domum," the Latin renderineof " Home, Sweet Home," was ren- dered by the students. A tea was afterwards partaken of in the senior students class room, and evensong was , given in the chapel at five o'clock. During the afternoon, specimens of needlework and drawings of the students, were on view in the junior students class room.
