"OPOSED NEW WATERWORKS FOR

ST. HELENS.

In answer to the Inspector, the Town-clerk (Mr Je.ves), said the Corporation had the lease with option of purchase of two acres of land on which they proposed to erect toe station. The lease was for five years from the Ist of July, 1892.. If the Board sanctioned the loan the Corporation would give notice of purchase immedi- ately. Mr. Gaskin (continuing) detailed the estimated expen- diture. He said £457 had been expended for trial bor- ing. They had sunk 402 ft. It was a pebble bed forma- tion of the new red sandstone all through. The water now stood ten inches above the surface level, and test- ing the quantity of water by all the available power they had then, they got 250,000 gallons in twenty-four hours. They proposed to put down two wells 150 feet deep and eleven feet diameter. From the bottom of one well they would start with a twenty-four inch borehole diminishing to eighteen inches., which was similar to those they had at Kirkby.and Knowsley. The borehole, would be 300 or 350 feet from the bottom of the well, making altogether a depth of about 500 feet. He did not think it was at all possible to strike brine at that depth. The cost of the two wells, including sinking and keeping the two wells clear of water during sinking operations would be £5,500. The cost of the pipe-line would be From Melling to Kirkby, £5,851 ; Kirkby to Knowsley, £9,280 ; and Knowsley to Brownedge. £28,550. The total estimate was £72,402, leaving £2,595 for contin- gencies. The pipeline would be of the best class of iron with leaden joints. By Mr. Sutton : "The tctal distance from the centre of St. Helens to Menke,* would be eleven or twelve miles. They would possibly drain the whole of the northern portion of the township of Melling. They might cause surface wells to dry up within a radius of half a mile, and other wells within a radius of one mile. The South- port well at Aughton had drained all the wells in that township. There was no good supply of water in the townships of Bickerstaffe or Simon's Wood. The Town Clerk said the Corporation having acquire.d laud could put it to any use they liked consistent with common law. They were' prepared to supply Melling with 5 per cent, of the water at Bd. per 1,000 gallons, that price being what St. Helens would have to • pay Liverpool if they wanted water from the City Council's mains. Mr. ftoscoe, of the West Derby Union, asked if the Corporation would give them the same terms as Orms- kirk. It transpired that West Derby was in the Liverpool limits, and the inspector said that put an end to the matter. Professor Hull, geologist, said he believed the minimum yield of the proposed wells would never be less than 1,000,000 gallons for twenty four hours, and would pos- . sibly go up to 1,580,000. He believed the Corporation were perfectly justified in spending £30,000 on these wells—in fact, it was an absolute necessity, and in his opinion they ran no risk. Ile did not think the South- port wells, which were two and a half miles away,would ne in the least affected, but the new well might drain ihe whole of Melling township. It would not, however, affect Bickerstaffe. Dr. John Robertson, water analyst to St. Helens Corporation, said the samples of the water were exceed- ingly pure—one of the purest it was possible to have for domestic supply. Ultimately the Ormskirk Rural Sanitary Authority ' agreed to the St. Helens Corporation tennis of Bd. per ' 1,000 gallons. Mr. Brownbill, landowner adjoining, thought he should have a tree supply ; and Mr. Roper, agent to the Earl of Lathom, said if that was entertained he would ask for the same consideration for several of his lord- ship's farms. The 'town Clerk said such requests might go on ad infitituin. but the arrangement arrived at with the local authority would cover all the adjoining landed pro- perty. The Inspector: Do you know the law on the subject of underground water ? The Town Clerk said he had explained that there were no rights to underground water ; and Mr. Brownbill said he was satisfied there was no op- position to meet. The Town Clerk thanked the inspector for his cour- tesy, and the proceedings terminated. Major-General Crozier then proceeded to Melling to the site.

POLITICAL LIBEL ACTION AT ST, :HELENS. A LIBERAL AGENT v. A LABOUR CANDIDATE. Political circles of St. Helens were considerably interested on,Wednesday in an action for libel arie- jug out of the recent municipal elections, heard by arrangement at the local county court, before his Honour Judge Shaud and a special jury. In the Con- test for the representation of the North Eccleston Ward the candidates were Mr. B. B. Glover (Conservative) and Mr. I. B. Rose (Liberal), and Mr. Charles Liptrot, landlord of the Beehive Hotel, Bridge-st., came forward as an Independent Labour candidate. Mr. Liptrot's two nomination paoe.s IMIPTP declared invalid on the ground that t 1 signed the papers of Mr. Js issued ad- dresses to the electors, in wilach he alleged that his nomination had been upset by dishonourable actions on the part of Mr. Andrew Muir, agent for Iffellose. These allegations formed the subject of Wednesday's action, Mr. Muir being the plaintiff, and Mr. C. Liptrot the de- fendarit. Other actions are pending in connection with the same matter. Mr. W. D. M'Conkey, of Liverpool, instructed by Mr. J. P. Mearns, appeared for the lain- tiff, and Mr. Thomas Swift, instructed by Mr. Joseph Massey, represented the defendant. Mr. M'Conkey, in opening the case,described Mr. Muir as a commercial traveller and election agent at munici- pal contests. Upon the appearance of the alleged libels the plaintiff had instructed his solicitor, Mr. illearns, to ask for a public withdrawal, and threatened proceedings against Mr. Liptrot for libel. Mr. Liptrot appeared to have consulted Mr. Massey upon the matter, for on the 4th November Mr. Massey wrote to Mr. Liptrot's solicitor.—" Some days ago Mr. Liptrot consulted me about improper prac- tices of your agent, Mr. Muir, which consists of Mr. Muir having published false statements of the withdraw- al of Mr. Liptrot as a candidate at the election for the purpose of procuring the election of Mr. Rose. My clients iwists that Mr. Muir shall acknowledge these and apolo- gise for them,otherwise proceedings will be taken against him." According to that communication the plea of justification was set up, and it was asserted that the statements of Mr. Liptrot were true in substance and in fact, if Mr. Liptrot could prove that to be the case, then he (Mr. M'Cenkey) would have no case against him ; but if he failed to satisfy the jury that his statements were true, then he asked them to award the plaintiff substantial damages. Mr. G. T. Lee, chief clerk in the Town Clerk's depart- ment, produced the nomination papers and forms of ob- jection handed in by Mr. Lamb at the Mayor's objection coirr. No written objection was handed in by Mr. Muir. Mr. Muir, the plaintiff, then gave evidence in support of his counsel's opening statement. He said the allega- tions complained of were absolutely untrue. Joseph Appleton, fruit and flower dealer, Westfield- street, deposed that before Mr. Muir called he had signed a paper on Mr. Liptrot's behalf. He had heard some one in conversation say that Mr Liptrot bad retired, and on that account he signed Mr. Rose's paper.—Mr. Swift: Did you tell Mr. Liptrot that you were busy in the shop va hen Mr. Muir called, and that if Mr. Muir had not given you to understand that lie (Mr. Liptrot) had re- tired, you would not have signed Mr. Rose's paper ? Witness : Yes, I did say that. • John Martindale, collier, Virgil-st., said he signed the nomination paper on Mr. Rose's behalf before he signed that of Mr. Liptrot. Mr. Swift, for the defence, submitted that Mr. Liptrot had been led to suppose that Mr. Muir had been guilty of the actions alleged against him. Mr. Thomas Glover, J.P., miner's agent, said lie acted as Mr. Liptrot's agent at the recent election. He de- tailed a visit to Mr.Bain's offics, where he saw Mr. Muir. Some nomination papers were on the table, and Mr. Muir said they were those of Mr. Fletcher. a candidate M Farr Ward. Mr. Muir took the nomination, paper in his (witness's) hand, looked it over.tand then handed it back, with the remark that he was the agent on Mr. Rose's behalf, and the nominations before him on the table were those of Mr. Rose. Mr. William Lamb, Conservative registration agent, depnsed that in conversation with the plaintiff the latter spoke of having" cooked Mr. Liptrot's goose," and that the plaintiff had asked him to put his (Mr. Muir's) objec- tions to Mr. Liptrot on his own paper and hand them in at one time. Mr. Charles Liptrot, the defendant, said that Mr. Joseph Appleton, who had signed both nominations papers,informed him that Mr. Muir had obtained his signature to Mr. Rose's paper by saying that Mr. Liptrot had gone to the wall and was out of the contest alto- gether. That was the impression on his (Mr. Appleton.s) mind or he would not have signed. He (defendant) thoroughly believed the statements in his addresses to the electors were true when he issued them.—ln answer to Mr. M'Conkey, defendant said that there were other persons whom Mr. Muir had spoken to in the manner complained of. His Honour, in summing up, said that the point for the jury to decide was the truth or otherwise of the allegations in the defendant's address that Mr. Muir had got two persons on the burgess roll to sign the papers for Mr. Rose after they had signed his (Mr. Liptrot's) papers. There could be no doubt that the addresses would hold a man up to comtempt and ridicule unless there was justification for their statements, and that was a question for the consideration of the jury. It was for them to say whether or not Muir obtained the signa- tures to Mr. hose's papers with the view of defeating Mr. Liptrot's candidature, and. if they thought so, then the defendant was justified in saying strong things about such scandalous conduct. The jury found a verdict for the plaintiff, with one farthing damages. His Honour gave iudgment accord- ingly, with costs on the scale above £2O.

The Medical Annual advises Practitioners to remember that when recommending COCOAS as food and beverage for lurslids, the name CADBURY on any:packet of Cocoa is a gu-rantee ot purity.—Adrt.

SOCIAL, POLITICAL AND L LTE EtA R I NOTES,

-- The disgraceful outrage in the French Chamber will receive the condemnation of all supporters of law and order throughout the whole of Europe. The unsettled state of french society makes it exceedingly regrettable that an occurrence of this character should take place at the present time, apart from other considerations of greater moment. !f no higher value is going to be set on Milian life than that of the Anarchist Valliant,- then it will be necessary to adopt repressive measures that will effect n desirable change. Organisations that, are banded together for an unlawful purpose' must be instantly suppressed, and the stern hand of the law make itself felt. It is pleasing to notice the kind expressions of sympathy with France from both political parties, and the British Government has added further testimony to its friendliness with , foreign powers whenever they are placed in an un- fortunate position through no fault of their own.

The activity of our Political representatives at West- minster is not proving conducive to the progress of public business. For a whole week they have been dis- cussing the question of Ecclesiastical and Parochial Charities as contained in Clause 13, of the Parish Coun- cils Bill. Very little good has been done by our " or- ganised,obstructionists," and if they will persist in carrying out the policy of talking for talking's sake, then the closure will have to be applied. It is now only a week from Christmas, when it was expected the measure would be passed, but the fight is being pro- longed and it is difficult to forecast the conclusion of the present session: It is, however, well that the public should know who are their friends, and the effort that is being made by Tory agitators to cut down the powers and duties of local representative bodies which are to be instituted under the Bill, will serve to remind the electors how vain are the Tory praises of the British democracy.

What is going to be done with the House of Lords? Either that body will have to be reformed or else become subservient to the wishes of the people. The easy 'man- ner in which they threw out the Home Rule Bill has encouraged them to do the same thing in other cases, and against the expressed wish of the House of Com- mons, they reject the" betterment " principle in the Bill promoted by the London County Council. This Council declares that without the principle of better- mant the Bill is useless, but because these hereditary legislators are opposed to it, they are allowed to thwart the good intentions of a popular body, who are anxious to aditist equitably and fairly the arno'int payable by propelty owners, who will gain very largely by the public improvements projected under the London County Council Bill. They apparently also intend to mutilate the Employer's Liability Bill by making it useless for practical purposes through the introduction of a " Contracting out Clause." But this kind of work will not last long as Lord Salisbury well knows.

, The bye-election which has been necessitated at Accrington through the appointment of Mr. Leese, Q.C., to the Recordership of Manchester is exciting consider- able interest throughout the country. Itis likely that it will be a three cornered contest, as the " Independent Labour Party" have adopted a candidate to ad vocato their particular policy, in addition to the Liberal and Tory candidates already in the field. It is a pity that the Labour Party are so blind to their interests as to endeivour to prevent the return of Mr. Leese, for such is the case, seeing that they cannot possibly hope to return their candidate. It is quite possible that a num- ber of Liberal voters will be attracted to this new stand- ard, and Mr. Hermon Hodge may through such a slivi- sion work his way in between OA two. The folly of taking this course was well illustrated at the last General Election, and if the " Independent Labour Party "still persists in taking such foolish action, it will bring its own reward, but certainly not of the kind they expect.

It is not expected that the discussion on the vexed question of the unemployed, in the Douse of Commons on Tuesday last, will be productive of any practical re- sults. Politicians of every class and creed deplore,quite as much as Mr. Keir Hardie, the fact of such a large number of men being out of work ; and if the problem could be solved everybody would be delighted. Unfortu- nately,there is no unanimity of agreement as to the best course to be adopted in the matter, and after much talk on the subject—some instructive, and part senseless— the Douse resumed its normal aspect. Though it is de- sirable that as much as can be done, should be at- tempted at the earliest possible moment, it is to be feared that the unemployed question will have to still be subjective to the laws of our recognised political eco- nomy. It is not a matter that can be understood all in a moment, and any ill-advised action on the part of the Government would be very much regretted throughout the country. •

lel Among ---- - RI- I riling people with a

the magazines which endeavour to supply euple with a healthy literature and of ood qua- lity, may be classed the "Young Man." While in the hands of Mr. F. A. Atkins a very fair level indeed was maintained, hut since he has been joined by the Rev.W. J. Dawson as co-editor the circulation has increased tremendously. The mature experience of Mr. Dawson together with his extensive knowledge of English litera- ture has proved invaluable to numbers of young men who hive had a desire to cultivate their intellectual faculties. The last volume which has just finished with the December number is certainly better than any that have preceded it, and to all young men who arein the least fond of good literature, they could not do better than invest in a copy, as its contents will always be fresh.

The first number of the new volume which will be ready about the end of this month promises to be equally good. It will contain stories by the Rev. Silas Hock ng and Mr. Robert Barr. An interview with the great novelist, Rider Baggard, is also promised for this number, which will also include an illustrated character-sketch ot Mr. Balfour, by that wellknown journali..t, Mr. H. W. Massingham. Dr. Parker will also contribute an article on " My first sermon," which will doubtless be of more than usual interest. It would be a serious omission in speaking of the "Young man" if no reference was made to the " Young woman, which is issued in conjunction with the former, and all the praise bestowed on the one may be claimed by the other. Mr. Dawson's papers. on " Half hours in the Library " though appearing in the " Young woman" have been heartily enjoyed by mem- bers of the opposite sex.

-- All the talk about Mr. Gladstone retiring from office on account of his age has now vanished, and our Grand Old Man has been appealed to by the " Times" not to guage the strength and spirits of our younger legislators by his own. The Prime Minister has borne up wonderfully under the strain of continuous sittings during the time the Home Rule Bill was in Committee, and he never seems to tire in anything like the time which tries much younger men. As if to refute the charge of decaying strength and mental power, we are now informed that M^. Gladstone is engaged on a translation of Horace. The undertaking of such an arduous task at his age fills us with amazement.

In last week's Literary World," Norman Gale ha an article on Mr. Richard Le Gallienne's new book " The ReLyon of a Literary Man." It is on the whole sympa- thetm and appreciative. Mr. Gale says " If the Religioa of a Literary Man does not soothe many a sad heart we never more will prophesy."

Some interesting particulars are also give of " Q whose real name is Mr. A. T. Quiller Couch. His chief books are " Naughts and Crosses" and " The Splendid Spur." His latest book entitled " That Delectable Duchy " is quite up to his former publications, and exceedingly well written.

Jerome's new Magazine Journal "To Day" seems to be doing well just now, but it is very questionable whether the present large issue will be maintained. He is apt to become slightly sarcastic in his " To-day " notes, other- wiselt is well worth reading. G. F. C.

PARDONED FQR NEGLECT O.F DUTY. ----- One day last winter a postman named Hutchinson, while engaged collecting letters on his round in Shef- field, saw a boy struggling in the Don where the river was deep anki coated withice. Plunging in, clothes and all, the brave man, after two minutes' immersion, found the boy ; and though the current bore them some dis- tance away, he at length reached the bank, and carried the lad home. He then got a cab man to take his bag to the Post Office with a letter, saying, " lam very sorry such a thing has occurred [alluding to his not being in a fit state to complete the collection], but I could not bear to see the lad struggling in the water, and nobody but myself te give him any assistance. I hope you will pardon me for my negligence of duty in such an instance as this." Pardoned ! Of course he was pardoned, and the Postmaster-General haVing referred the case to the Royal Humane Society, that body gave the heroic post- man a medal. All things considered, however, that seems hardly a sufficient reward for such a noble action —so modestly done, too.—From " Little Folks."

We learn that in consequence of the retirement, through ill-health, of the Senior Partner, Mr. T. G. Lloyd, of Messrs. Sales, Pollard & Co., Tobacco, Cigar and Snuff Manufacturers, of Farringdon Street, London, E.C., this old established business has been taken over by the celebrated firm of Tobacco Manufacturero, Messrs. W. D. &H. 0. Wills, Limited. It is intere,Ling to note that Messrs. Sales, Pollard & Co., havim.,, carried on their business for over 150 years, are the oldest 'Po- bacco Manufacturers in London, while Messrs. W. D. & 11. 0. Wills, Limited, is the oldest established firm in the United Kingdom. they having been in existence for close upin two centuries.

A. ST. HELENS RAILWAY MYSTERY.

--- A REMARKABLE ACCIDENT TO A MASTER wIIEILLWRIGHT. An inquest touching the death of John Tyldesley, master wheelwright, of Little Ilulton, near Bolton, who met with injuries on the 9th September, on the London and North-Western Railway at St. Helens, was held by Mr. Butcher on Monday. Mr. Bland, from the Solicitors' Department, Lime-street, represented the railway company, and Mr. Fernihough appeared for the relations—Richard Tyldesley, son of the deceased, said his father had told him that he thought he must have been getting in the train while in motion. He felt a regular twist and could not recollect anything.—Mr.Thomas Howarth, lime merchant, Bicker- staffe-street, said tlie deceased called en the 9th Septem- ber at twenty minutes to four to see if he had any orders. He left in a waggonette for Sutton, three miles from St. Helens. He was then sober. Witness saw him in the hospital, but he did not mention anything about the accident. Deceased had an excursion ticket from Manchester to St. Helens.—William Dooley, Fenton-st., St. Helens,chemical labourer, said he found the deceased in the six-foot on the level crossing near to Itoherts's Copper Works, St. Helens, and along-side ut Ihe St. Helens and Sankey Canal. It was 200 yards from St. Helens Station. Witness asked him what he was doing, I and he said he was going to Walkden. He was stunned. I Witness saw that his head was cut, and three fingers of I the left hand had been crushed off. There was also a bad wound on the fleshy part of his leg.—By the Jury : There were no lights at the crossing, and men could be knocked down by passing trains. There was no one in charge of the crossing at night. There were no gates to prevent anyone crossing the lines. The crossing was a public thoroughfare from the main road to a number of works and collieries. It was, also the towing pathof the canal.—By the Coroner : The crossing WAS not on the road to or from Sutton.—Mr. Richard Boardman, stationr.,aster, St. Helens, said he did not see the deceased at all on the night he was injured. The Man- chester train left at nine o'clock, but lie did not know whether that was the excursion train. None of the porters saw a man riding on a footboard. Witness did not think there was any right of road over the crossing. —The Coroner thought it would be the wisest course for the jury to return a verdict that deceased was found at the crossing, having sustained the injuries by a passing train. Their verdict would not bind either the railway ' company or the relatives. THE STATE OF ELLESMERE-STREET.

Sir,—l would like to ask through your valuable paper whether it is not time that some steps were taken by the Paving Committee to alter the prc-ent deplorable state of Ellesmere-street. It is both a public disgrace and a source of danger too,that it should be left open as a public road in its present state. A few evenings ago, a rev. gentleman, who was applinted to preach at the mission chapel in Eilesmerc-street, found his way to the bottom of Greenall-street and Ellesmerc stet, but had to beat a retreat on account of the awful state of the road, and make a circuit round Church-street and Mer- sey-street before L log able t I get to the mh-ion. Such a state of things ought not to be, considering that this is the nearest road from Howley Quay to Mersey-street and the surrounding neighbourhood, and that the work- men and work-women use it mostly on account of its nearness of access to the works in that neighbourhood. Something ought to be done to move the Committee in this matter. I remain yours. ONE OF THE SUFFERERS. Warrington, Dec. 14, 1893.

WARRINGTON SUNDAY SCHOOL UNION.

Sir,—With your kind permission I would like to draw the-attention of all interested in Sunday School work to the recently formed Warrington Sunday School Union, and to make an earnest appeal to all schools, holding and practising the doctrine as embodied in rule "2," to become identified with the Union. This rule is as fol- lows:—" The Union shall compri- 3 those schools within a radius o! five miles, which hold the doctrine of the Trinity ; the atonement of our Lord Jesus Christ; the regenerative influence of the Holi Spirit; and that all Scripture is given by inspiratinn of God. The ministers, officers and teachers of all connected schools shall be re- garded as members of the Union." There is great need for more combined and systematic effort in our Sunday School teaching, and work gener- ally, and the formation of such an institution as the one referred to—which is strictly of an undenominational character, and which will, if worked aright, offer many advantages to schools connected—should be a matter of satisfaction to those who take an interest in the religi- ous education of the young. A hearty invitatien is held out to the ..^bools in the town and district, and any information may be had on application to the secretary.—Yours truly, S. FEKNLEY, 14, Sankey-st., Warrington.

THE PRINCIPLES OF GOVERNMENT.

Sir,—Mrs. John Bidclulph Martin, who, as Victoria Woodhull, caused a commotion in American political circles by standing for the Presidency twenty years ago, has recently elaborated a system of Government which she terms the " Humanitarian "system. This system is to be, not Liberal or Conservative, not Socialistic or Individualistic—but simply Humanitarian; which, defined, means a Government which shall, in all ' respects, look after the internal welfare of its people. Es' the laissez faire school of politicians it will doubtless be objected that men are quite capable of looking after their own welfare ; but the existence of one confirmed and irresponsible drunkard is an undeniable proof to the contrary. It may well be argued, indeed, that if Gov- ernments have passed Game Laws, have introduced '4-actory Acts, have placed Criminal Law Amendment Acts upon the Statute hook, why should they not go further and make it penal, as is now suggested,for those who have not sufficient means to live properly, or for those afflicted with an hereditary disease, such as con- sumption,to marry and so perpetuate,if not increase,the sum of human misery ? Mrs. Martin's definition of the Cabinet is interesting : " In a Humanitarian Government the Cabinet would be composed of Philosophers representing every branch of Science. They would utilize this knowledge and would feel how necessary it is to look after the internal welfare of the people." This is an interestitig question, and one quite above chose of party politics.—Yours faithfully, TERTULLIAN.

CHRIST AND THE CATHOLICS.

Sir,—Will you permit me to point out to" Dissenter" that as Catholics do not admit the Bible to be the only rule ot faith they are not bound to prove everything by it. The Church itself is older than the Bible and is the witness for it ; and if the Church's authority is sufB- - to decide what books shall or shall not be included in the Bible, why is it not sufficient also to decide what doctrines or practices shall be taught. The "worship "of the Mother of Christ and the yen- eration of the relics of good men, are the result of the ordinary feelings of humanity and need no defence ; we, like the first disciples,wish to persevere with one mind In prayer with "Mary the Mother of Jesus and with His brethren" (Acts 1,14), and to " glorify God "in His Saints (Gal.i, 24). The means and manner of so doing differ with the circumstances in which we live. The power to give" indulgencies " is derived from the authority to remit sins given to the Church in the begin- ning (Matt. xviii, 18). " Confession "is commanded to. all (James v, 16) ; whether it is public, before the whole congregation, or " auricular" to 4e priest only, depends on the judgment of the Church as to which is the more convenient or useful system. Do our Protestant Dissen- ters practice any system of open confession of this ? Special rules for the clergy are laid down in the Pastoral Epistles—e.g.,they must not have been married , more than once—so that they really were a separate class ; and as to the Apostles, the types of all, the won.l " separate "is used (Acts xiii, 2). The Church lays " no command of celibacy "upon.us ; it upholds marri- age as a sacrament. The Pope lithe successor of St. Peter, and,St. Peter is certainly distinguished from all the other'Apostles, in the New Testament. It is, for example, impossible to understand the Epistle to the Galations unless we recognise the position of primacy and authority svhleli he held amongst the first Christians. The Bible therefore, does witness, to those who study it attentively, to the truth and propriety of those doctrines which the Catholic Church teaches and has always taught.—Yours truly, B. L. Dec. 7, 1893.

Sir,—The stand which " Dissenter " takes in matters religious would be an admirable one but for two or three rather important defects. His main thesis appears to be this : that no doctrine which is not explicitly set forth in Holy Writ is t. be accepted as part of God's revelation. This,indeed,is the contention of Protestants in general, but in attempting its demonstration no Pro- testant has succeeded as yet in satisfying all the require- ments. of logic. - • " • • •

_ In the first place,what proof have we that those hooks which Protestants admit as Scripture are the only in- spired writings that ever came into existence? In his first Epistle to the Corinthians, St. Paul speaks, ,is we may reasonably infer, of a letter which he already had sent to them. What has become of it? If it was not an inspired writing, what proof have we of the fact? If it was inspired, who shall say that it contained nothing which cannot he found in such of his Epistles as had COMO down to us. or in some other part of that volume ' which we call the Bible? 'rhere is good reason to think, moreover, that St. Paul wrote an epistle to the Lancli- , ceans. For all that Protestants can show to the contrary, may not this also have been iuspired and have contained

THE EXAMINER, SATURDAY, DECEMBER 16, 1893.

this Public Hall (I mean Town Hall—they have changed the name)—is going to cost. Of course it is said the subject is "Old and thread-bare" and it should drop. Well, I like the maxim "forgive and forget." Yet when I get tim quarterly water accounts with double to pay, and the other Commissioners' accounts with one-fourth more to pay than formerly, I sigh for the good old times when Mr. McCorquodale was Chairman, and also the times of Mr. Gooch who followed the lines of his pre- decessor; and I then say, if in my power, I would, with one or two exceptions, make a clean sweep of the present Board, for it would be impossible for the next one to be worse. _ _

Mr. Evans is a friend of mine, but I object to his prin- ciple of levelling up the rates, and also his plan of free education, even with the assistance of a lady; and as to the actions of the Chairman, let me direct your atten- tion to a retrospect of some of the things recently done by him. A: When the deputation met the Commis- sioners at the beginning of last month to ask their assist- ance to get the township divided into wards, the Chair- man told it that he had not consulted any of his col- leagues and could give no reply that day. Yet Mr. Stone's letter on the ward question had been sent some six weeks , before. B : He told the deputation that he considered INewton well represented, and that Mr.. Evans was a I Newton representstive, and, although the expressions of dissent were general and figures were produced to show that Mr. Evans was not a Newton representative,he (the Chairman) said hiA case was proved. C: The Press was excluded at the special meeting the Commissioners had to discuss the application for wards, and to this Jay we have no details as to how the question was handled, beyond an imaginative report. D: At last meeting ha is reported to have Said "Whether the County Council had power to divide the township into wards and in- crease the number of memt W ,14 a Puestion he was unable to form a judgmeot on." Th: la is a most remarkable statement. Why did he not ask "our Law Clerk," who was at his right hard? The question has been before the Chairman for some three months; the Board has a "gal adviser who attends the meetings regularly, and yet the Chairman tells the ratepayers through the " Examiner " that he does not know the County Councils' powers in this 'mportant matter. Surely our Law Clerk should have been al'owed to make a public statement on the question? I venture to tell the Board and the ratepayers generally that the County Council have full power in this matter. It has been asked to exercise those powers, and the present Board of Com- missioners cannot prevent it. Yet it would have been so much better if the Board had acted in a conciliatory spirit instead of throwing obstacles in the way, in the manner it has done. Crewe was for many years governed by the Railway Company quite as much as this town is to-day. Yet after many struggles and rebuffs it succeeded in getting freedom. The last deputation who attended the share- holders half-yearly meeting at Euston, at which various speakers laid the question before the shareholders, had the desired effect. Poor Sir Richard Moon almost cried and begged the meeting to leave the matter jn his hands, which it did. After that the railway representa- tives got the hint, and they quietly but gradually resigned. It has a soothing effect to think the ratepayers are awakening up—the signs are unmistakeable—with a de- termination to throw off the yoke; and why should they not succeed ? The first step has been made. The Ward Committee was unanimously selected and delegated with full powers to use all lawful means to obtain the wards. A stronger or more representative Committee could not have beers appointed, and from what I know individually of its members I am sure no stone will be left unturned to obtain what is so much needed as, yours respectfully, December 12th, 1893.

FREEDOM.

hoods," &c. Well, Sir the opinion of most people in War- rington is, that with Alderman Harrison the afore-sal " genius for inaccuracy" was born fully developed, and that in his case it has for a great number of years answered "all the purposes of deliberate falsehoods." I venture to think, Sir, even yet, that my reputation for truthfulness will bear favourable comparison with the character of Alderman Joseph Harrison. Certainly if the people of Warrington were called upon to select a local imitator of the great " George Washington, who never told a lie," their ehoice•would not fall upon this particular" Joseph." With that sublime disregard of facts which is so nrominent a feature in the personality of Alderman Harrison, but which I now presume is merely an evidence of his "genius for inaccuracy "—"I thank thee, Jew, for teaching me that word "—or at the worse of his " crazy vapouring," he tells us that the opposition to this scheme of pulling down the Park wall is purely political, barring the aid of a " few erratic Conservatives."

I haNte care,ally examined the records of the Council proceedings since 1884, and I 11nd that at various times the whole ef the following Conservative members of the Council have voted against the removal of the wall, viz,, Messrs. Webster, Davies, Litton, Chandley, Ashton, Jas. Fairclougk, Tinsley, Percival, J. V. Taylor, W. H. Jen- kins, Bolton and Heaton. Several of these gentlemen are no longer with us, but although they were loyal and active members of the Conservative party, they were strenuously opposed to the needless expenditure of the ratepayers' money for the purpose of improving the property of the Tory club. Talk about a "dirty, low, political job " (thanks again Mr. Harrison), why, what else is it ? The advocacy of this question has not originated with the able and more independent mem- bers of the Tory party in the Council, but has been left almost entirely in the hands of a faction who do not scruple to take advantage of their present majority to force a question upon which the opinion of the rate- payers has never been sought, and which when the hill comes to be paid will probably cost them dear. If the Conservative Club had not been situated where it is, the raking down of this wall would have been advocated " when the pigs begin to 11y," and not before. But, Sir, what is the tenible sum of my offence that Alderman Harrison should thus wring his hands, shriek out his maledictions, and genera'ly make a silly of himself ? I did not say a single word at the last Council meeting against the idea of improving the ap- pearance of the Town Hall by taking down the wall. I simply did what I believed to be my duty to the rate- payers whose servant I am by opposing an expenditure which at the present time is wholly unnecessary and most inopportune. Surely I had a right to do this without incurring the " wild and crazy vapourings " of Alder- man Harrison. At any rate the members ot the Council did not take exception to my conduct, for they paid me the highest compliment which any speaker can receive, by listening with the most respectful attention to the whole of my remarks. It is not material to my argu- ment whether the " job " costs £4OO or £2,ooo,although no one but a fool would dream for a moment that the former sum will cover what is contemplated, and what will bathe consequence nt the removal of the wall which will have to be &tie sooner or later.

The present is a most serious time in the history of our town. With a debt of considerably over half a million sterling, of which no less than £lOO,OOO is for unre- munerative improvements, with rates at 63. 4d. in thee with an unpaid balance of expenditure on smallpox account of nearly £3,000 carried forward for payment out of next year's rate, and, therefore, a great probab- ility of the present excessive rates being maintained for at least one more year. with bad trade and little pros- pect of an early improvement, with the excessive poverty and distress which will follow for months, vet in the train of the recent sad lock-out in the coal trade, with certain items of additional heavy expenditure con- fronting us, and which cannot possibly be much longer delayed, with the improvement of Bridge-st., not half accomplished, I do not hesitate to denounce any expen- diture which is not absolutely unavoidable.

There is not a solitary argument that will bear ex- amination which can be adduced in favour or forcing this scheme at the present moment, and the combined influences of the " wild and crazy vapourings " of Alder- man Harrison, and that inborn "genius for Inaccuracy" with which he is so richly endowed, will fail to throw dust in the eves of any ratepayer who is not already blinded by party prejudice as to the real object of the promoters of the present wanton expenditure of public money.—Yours, &c.,

HENRY ROBERTS.

DISESTABLISHMENT AND HISTORY.

Sir,—lt is lamentable to see the ostrich policy pursued by the State Church stipendiaries, supported by,amprigst their own hooded victims, members of the various free churches, of all people, who sign themselves " Dissen- ter," &c., and backed up by Frodsham folks, and kindred ilk. This people would give us to understand that no association, whatever it laws or regulations may be, has the right to censure or expel its members who become obnoxious and break them.

The Catholic Church, for example, if it expels any member who is a traitor in the camp ; the Good Team- lard, if they discharge one who persistently indulges in diurnal dram drinking ; the Protestant " Evangelical " alliance, if it finds one of its adherents accepting a bribe from the Catholics to write or spout in favour of " Popery," and thereupon dismisses him as a black sheep ; all those bodies are intolerant, persecutors, chaining down, and enslaving the human intellect, and all sorts of horrible enemies to " liberty " and " free- do:n." Such is the philosophy now being gravely advanced for the mental digestion of grown men, in the epistolary columns of the" Examiner. . . .

The " 'Protestant Reformed religion as established by law "is the legal title of those churches in this kingdom which are subject to the yoke of Caesar, or the civil power ; in airect violation of the injunctions laid down by our Lord himself in His remarks on the coin of the tribute. It matters hot whether it takes the form of Anglican Episcopalianism, or Scottish Presbyterianism, inaunuch as the sovereign, at the coronation service,has to swear to maintain that Caesarian, or civil yoked religion before the Archbishop of Canterbury can place the crown on his (or her) head. That law-established religion, allied and subject to those very " principalities and powers" against which Our Saviour established the crusade of ills church militant, His kingdom " not of this world," does not date further back than theßeform- ation.

According to a Parliamentary return obtained by the late Mr. Summers, M.P., more than five-sixths of the income of this Cmiarian religion arises from pre-reform- ation endowments. At no period in pre-reformation times was there ever in this country such a law estab- lished religion. The church in those days was as free from state control as are the Nonconformist Churches of to-day. No doubt the Pope wielded immense influence, for the obvious reaton that the whole of the people were Catholics. But so also would the president of the Wea- levan Conference, or the moderator of the Scottish Free Church wield immense influence if the whole of the British people were to join (eau •of their respective churches. The church endowments, then, both pre and post-reformation, are private endowments, with this important distinction, tllat more than five-sixths of them are now misappropriated to totally different pur- poses from those for which they were bequeathed ; and this misappropriation is held purely in virtue of the Act of Royal bumemacy in matters spiritual, which was first thrust on an unwilling people in 1534, A.D. It is true that latterly a new superstition has been invented by this Cmsarian sect, called "Anglican Con- tinuity," and a hired band of itinerant lecturers is engaged to preach the superstition. Its object, is to prove, by ths falsification of history, that the present department of state known legally as "The Protestant Reformed Religion as established by Law," and the pre- Reformation Church are identical. If John Bull, along with his brethren, Sandy and Taffy (Pat, wiser than we, dissolved partnership with us in this incubus more thari 20 years ago), can only be. like Gulliver's Apothecary, gulled into swallowing this proposition, compensation for disendowtnent is secured, and disestablishment will be conceded with a light heart. Unfortunately for the success of this deal, we have had in operation since the Irish church compensation farce, seventy-two years of a compulsory education law, and John Sandy, and Taffy are fully familiar with the historical fact that the pre-Reformation endowments were left to a totally different church, and for quite different purposes from those of the present " trustess," viz.,'interalia, housing, feeding, and clothing the poor, tending the sick, instructing the ignorant, masses for the dead, and all the other corporal and spiritual works of mercy ; in short for essentially national purposes. The fact is emphasised on their attention, inasmuch as they find themselves mulcted in poor rates, education rates, hospital rates, asylum rates, Scc., from all of which im- pnars,‘ previous to the advent of this alien sect (in- troduced into the country by German interlopers, and, according to historians Green, Hallam, and others, thrurt on the people by hired foreign bayonets), the English people were free. These things are matters of common knowledge,and it behoves all friends of freedom to rally round to the active support of their cause and not lose themselves in swelling the abusive" no popery' howlings of the " Evangelical " Alliance emissaries' which" Evangelical" Protestant Alliance show is run by " low "State churchmen, who are living on the bread of the State institution, in other words on the bread which belongs to the poor. It is too much after the con- duct of Nero, who, we are told,was busy fiddling whilst his capital was burning. The ignorant bogey of " Popery," which served so well in days gone by to scare away investigation, has vanished before the beams of modern enlightenment as completely as the ramparts of Jericho fell under the echoes of the bugle, and the " defenders " of the Cresarian church can no more retard the tide of Dis - establishment than King Canute could keep back the tide of the ocean.—Faithfully s. your WM. F. COMBER. Knowles Hill, Lewisham.

"HOME RULE AND TIIE PROTESTANT MINORITY."

Sr.—As I have three gentlemen to answer this week, I tre:,t you will kindly allow me a little extra space to du so. As " Old Churchman's" letter comes first on the list we will take him first. This gentleman in trying to refute my statement concerning the late Dean McNeile, practically admits that what I stated was true. To be sure he tries to explain it away, by saying that some Catholic girl took her master's child to a Catholic priest to be baptized without the knowledge of its parents, which is a very likely story indeed, and as old Shake- bacon would say "very like a whale " ; but he admits that the Dean did advise his people to be careful about the characters of the servants they took into their em- ploy, and the Dean being such a rabid Protestant, would (and did) take good care, so far as he could prevent it , that no Catholic girl would have an opportunity of get- ' ting a situation in any Protestant family. One has only I to read between the lines, to see that my assertion is more tharNproved by our excellent friend, "An Old Churchman," and I beg to thank him for the kind ser- vice (unwittingly no doubt) he has done me. And now number two is "A Dissenter," and he heads his letter "Christ and the Catholics "—(not Roman) thanks. Now, this gentleman is most profuse in his abuse' and in his reckless simile, he likens the Statims of the Cross to his boot. Well, judging by his high class letter, I have no doubt that he knows more about leather than the ques- tion he was so very foolish to write about. But sour correspondent is not of the nervous kind, he is -bold enough to write that Luther did not make the Protes- tant religion, and also that the Catholic Church was not made by our Blesezd Saviour Jesus Christ. Well, really, after this what next? Some of these milk-and-water historians and Bible class theologians, will try to per- suasle us historians, this is not the nineteenth century, and that we are all fools. Let us take the position of the Estab- lished Church of England regarding this question. A time was when "certain wholesome and godly exhor- Wiens " from the Book of Homilies were inevitable in England "every Sunday and Holy-day." It was thought that Englishmen could not have too much of that book for "quieting of their consciences " ; though they had , heard it "plainly and distinctly" from cover to cover. Their dread sovereign, Elizabeth, required the same to be "repeated and read again." It was privileged—an ex- ception to the general rule against " vain repetitions.' After the damning verdict of the fourteenth Homily pronounced on all previous churchmen,viz., that "laity and clergy, learned and unlearned, all ages, sects and degrees of men, women and children of whole Christen- dom had berm at once drowned in abominable idolatry, and that by the space of eight hundred years and more" it is not clear how such men as " Dissenter," are going to prove who (if not Christ Himself) created or ma le the Catholic religion. Now, we have got an illtistration above of the position of "church people" on this ques- tion. Now what is the position of our friends the Dis- senters? Did they ever claim to be the original church? No. They all of them maintain that—" The church of Rome became corrupt, after a certain time, and that, God rased up men to reform His church ''—that is the position of both Churchmen and Dissenters. But no sane man ever yet claimed that any other church was made by Christ excepting the Catholic church in com- munion with "the Apes olic ts 3 of Rome." (Vide, Rev. Cha=. Gore at the Church Congress). But they all I 'maintain that she fell into error. The Est ablished Church asserts that the Catholic Church was pure at first, and they profess to accept the first four councils of the church I as teaching a true and holy doctrine. Now what can be urged ir defence of the Catholic Church in communion with the see of Peter? First let us examine the charter given to her by our Divine Master, Jesus Christ. Now, I trust all those who have bibles will open them and read the following texts. The teaching church is called by St. Paul " theipillar and,groUnd of truth." (Ist Timothy, iii. 15.) Our Lord promises that " the gates of hell shall not prevail against His church." (St. Matt., xxviii. 20.) Also that the Holy Spirit shall abide with her for ever for the express purpose of guiding her into all truth : " I will ask the Father, and He shall give you another Paraclete, that He may abide with you for ever." (St. John, xiv, 16.) Our Lord Jesus Christ put the Apostles in His place in His divine mission upon earth, and in the office of teaching. "He says "As my Father hath sent me, I also send you." (St. John, xx. 21.), Again, in another place lie says, " Be that heareth you, heareth Me; and he that des- piseth you despiseth Me." St. Luke x. 16. "Go ye therefore teach all nations and 10, I am with you to the end of the world." St. Mdthew xxviii, 19-20. " Thou art Peter, and upon this rock I will build my church,and the gates of hell shall not prevail against it," St. Matthew xvi, 18. Now, it is certain that if the Catholic Church became corrupt, and if it was necessary to make a new church instead of it, then the gates of hell have prevailed against it. But Christ promised that this should never happen. SO that before Protestants can defend their new church, they must show that the pro- mise of Christ has not been kept, and that the devil has prevailed against the old church. 1 will leave this task to our friend " Dissenter." _ .

And now for Mr. Foulkes. This gentleman calls upon me to apologise to him or pay a forfeit to the infirmary I Well, now, look you—as our good Welshmen would say--there is nothing like cheek. For what shall I apologise? Mr. Foulkes makes a false asstrtion, saying that Cardinal Wiseman took an oath to persecute heretics. I ventured to deny this—and what evidence does this person bring to defend his statement. Ile says several persons headed by the false prophet Cumming, who beat old Mother Shipton in the prophe- tical business, saw the docuwnt. Well now Mr. Foulkes, what is this to do with our good Warrington public? Why can't you let them see a copy of the document in the original Latin and let them judge for themselves? And ' if you can't translate it I will try and get it done for you. I am always anxious to oblige anyone in difficul- ties. The cock and bull story from Spain is something like our American stories, highly flavoured. Such

stories always come from Spain. Now, if Mr. Foulkes and the rest of the McCrankies who go to make up the Evangelizal (Them!) Union, had to pay forfeit for every lie they utter about the Holy Catholic Church they would he very soon in the Union—not Evangelical, especially the soup—although by the way the workhouse , is an Evangelical institution. It is about the only return 1 that the English people have ever or are ever likely to get from the proceeds of the robberies committed by the reforming gentry in this country. I notice Mr. Foulkes trots outDr.Littledale's grotesque notion of "the unani- mous consent of the Fathers." Yet a little while, and Mr. Foulkes would denounce Dr. Littledale and all the Ritualists as " Jesuits in disguise," " traitors to the church," Romanists " Ste., &c.; and I verily believe that if this class of " tolerant " protestants only had their way, they would never rest till they had such good and earnest men as Rev. Bell-Cox, or our good charitable neighbour Rev. F. Slater, decently hung and buried in lime within the precincts of one of her Majesty's prison walls. Such are the ways of your "Evangelical toter- . ant Protestant." lam not ambitizms, and do not wish to engage any longer with such a mild, meek, militant, churchman, as this Mr. Foulkes. I like honest dealing, and fora man who can use Littledale one day, and then brand him as a traitor the next, I 'have nothing but Sympathy.—Yours, kc.,

J. C. DALTON.

"CARDINAL WISEMAN'S OATH."

Sir,—The points to be observed concerning the episco. pal oath are as follows :-1 : Before the Reformation, when England was a Catholic country, heresy was a crime against the law of the land. The legislature con- sidered it to be the duty of the bishops to arraign teachers of heresy before the church courts, and after regular trial, to excommunicate them or deliver them to the civil law. 2 : It is childish ignorance of Protestants to render the word " persoquar " by the modern word persecute in the case referred to. Its Latin meaning is " to follow up," "to pursue," and as Sinith's, one of the most standard Latin dictionaries, gives it to prose- cute, to take proceedings against. The oath of pre-reformation bishops contained the clause" Heretics and schismatics and rebels to our said lord, and his said succ-ssors,l will to the utmost powe prosecute and contend ageinst " Q guar et impurr- nato), her-use it was an ordirary and I al part of a bishop's duties to try cc ;of her -y in the church courts. 3 : Now that England is no longer a Catholic but a missionary country. and when heresy is no longer a crime which our bishops can make cognizable by the civil law, the clause I 's no longer any meaning in the sense of a public and legally recognized procedure. 4 : In view of the totally different condition of things now obtaining in England, Pope Pius VII. in 1818 (12th April) drew up and prescribed a form of episcopal oath to be taken by bishops of the British Em- pire. This form is the one now used at all consecrations of Catholic nishops in these count': . It is the same mainly as the old pre-Reformation oath, but with the clause about heretic', &c.,as al ye, complrt2ly left out. 5: At Cardinal Wiseman's consecration,a rding to the decree of Pius VII., this was the form of oath he took. 6: Qpnsequently, what Mr.Foulkes and other Protestant accusers have to prove is :-1 : That in C- dinal W'se- man's consecration, the decree of Pope Pius VII. was violated and infringed. 2: That the clause was not erased, and that it was publicly recited by him at his consecration in spite of the Papal decree. If they have any witnesses to prove this they ought to produce them. In conclusion, let me say that Mr. Foulkes would do well to study a little some other kind of educational ,--orks than "Jewel, Blackeney, and the charitable Dean."—Yours,&c., J. C. DALTON.

THE IMMORALITIES OF INDUSTRIALISM. ' Sir,—Whatever shade of doubt I might have had as to my being right in contending against the sinfulness—the , injustice—of intimidation has novr been removed. The amount of abuse I am getting is evidence that the medi- cine I have been offering is salutary, but bitterly dis- tasteful. Had I cared to covet popular favour, I should have advocated some easy, pleasant abrogation of ethical principles standing in the way of federated action and delegate interests. The "Down Grade" is the most acceptable, and its advocates are ever the in ost .opular. I should not have troubled you again on this question had there not been a fresh outbreak of the " 'orny 'ended" subversion of ethical principles to (supposed) class interests. I should have thought Mr. Whitham would have been sufficiently " level headed " to have seen that I was contending in favour of ill-paid lat ,ur if against more highly remunerated work. In othert words, I object to see the unemployed or badly paid' workers punished for the (apparent) advantage of a class of workers better situated every way. Why should the hearth of the very poor be cold in order that the miner shall have the means of riotous living ? And why shall these very poor not have the chance of work refused by these miners ? Again, the very poor among us still further suff,ir from the eff: •ts of tf,js sel- fish strike by the loss of British trade ensuing up:a it as an inevitable consequence. It appears to me that when we get a little more "Federated "we shall require a re- vision of the Decalogue. " Thou shalt not steal"—except the bread from the mouths of ether workers ! " Thou shalt not kill "—except to further the interests of the Federation ! It is a pity that men, in straining their eyes upon the end to be obtained, should lose sight of iniquity of the rteans employed to secure it. And it will be a thousand th .ies if the workers of this country accept the dogmas of " fanatic" who advises them to do this! The ultimate result can only be to their own dis- advantage. To show this let us look at the latest utter- ance of this Labour Prophet—who,however, regrets that he is not yet sufficiently " cranked " to be a thoroughly acceptable one—not that he need despair. He is an the highway to full'qualification. Listen ! "For instance, he (that's me) says that every man has to sell his labour in the best market at the best possibie " There- fore," says the " fanatic,"" the members of the Miner Federation had the right to get the best possible I rxe fo their labour." Quite so; Oh ! most profitless of Pro- phets ! Have I not repeatedly said so? But I wish it to dawn upon that zealot's brain of yours that there ' is a wide difference between the refusal to work your ; self and the refusal to let others work in your stead. The 1 former you have a perfect right to do—the latter is both unlawful and immoral. Take an illustration of the working of this doctrine. I The navvies employed on the Mersey Tunnel got some- thing less than half, a colliers wages, they worked under great disadvantages a rod physical discomfort—wet through most of their time. Let us suppose this strike and that job to have been on at the same time. Woulri not these men have been quite justified in leaving their job and offering to get coal at an advance on their own wages? And would not the masters have been justified In accepting them? And would it not have been the plain duty of the country to have defended, these men from molestation for so doing ? As a matter of " Com- mon Justice," yes Any attempt to bolster up artificial labour prices have, as antagonists, the whole unemployed labour, of the world. What hope then of its maintenance in face of such odds ? Finally, I most heartily endorse Mr. Price's assertion; "Man requires something more than work in this old world ; time for self-culture—mental and physi- cal—also tor the society of his wife and children ; ' but Ile should net trample on the prostrate form of his bro- ther in order to attain these requisites.—Truly yours, JOHN PEERS,

NOTES ON GAMES AND

ATHLETICS.

RUGBY _FOOTBALL.

Our local fifteen a-r-e-Paistiia-iiii;el;.way with singular expedition up the statistical ladder of the Lancashire Cup Competition. Indeed,certain experts who witnessed their performance against Swinton on Saturday, have delivered themselves of the opinion that the Wirepullers have a fine chance of carrying away the handsome goblet. It has been said of the results of the competition matches on Saturday that Fortune smiled with singular sweet- ness on several clubs,and bestowed her favours in unex- pected directions. "But, she is a fickle jade, and as inconsistent as the moon." Very, very, probably. At any rate, in the case of Swinton she tonk little heed of human expectations and flouted all amticipations witts a dainty twist of her shapely hand. These,of course,were the anticipations of the Swintonians.

However, there is no doubt that in this match some- thing else besides luck bad a hand in the results. Merit was the great umpire. And although the game was a draw, it has been generally admitted that Warrington had by far the best of the play, despite the fact that for the greater portion of the time they were a man short, and he above all men, one of the indomitable Bates— Jack. There was a very considerable muster of War- ' ringtonians on the field, and they made their presence I very evident at times when Warrington were experienc- I ing hard lines. The great feature of the game was the half back play of the Warrington couple, and their ex- positions were very excellent. But what a crushing criticism did one of our contemporaries pass upon them! Said the writer:—" These young gentlemen have all reputations of the most resplendent character, and what any one of them does not know about unscrupulous piracy and freebooting nractices the others cannot add to his knowledge. They were engaged on Saturday in exhibitiag their accomplishments to the satisfaction of themselves and to the depreciation of the game." R ither hard ; but still, it is somewhat sweetened, for, resumes the critic :—" I can, however,compliinent the Warring- ton halves on their exposition. They were smarter than their opponents, and,in all the subtle movements which have come to be recognised as the acme of halt back play they were eminently distinguished. Smack Bate was at his best, and got possession of the ball fre- quently."

Heesom was in fine form at back, fielding the leather in a grand style, though his kicking was a little faulty probably because he requires a little more weight. All the forwards were in remarkably fine form. At three quarter plav,however there was no particularindividual brilliancy shown. It would be well by the way for Carey to pay more attention in future to the demand of his fellow three quarters. Be was very negligent in re- cognizing Massey's speed, when it might have been of much service.

ANTECEDENTS.

This week our sketch is one of the oldest players in the Warrington Football Club, and yet one of the youngest. This may seem paradoxical, but it will be explained when it is stated that " Bill " Nevins is only 26 years of age, and yet he has played with the first team for eight years. he being a playing member of the first team when only 18 years of age. It is about twelve years since, when Nevins commenced his football career. His first train- ing ground- was the now defunct St. Elphin's Rugby f xitball club, whose field was at Howley. Nevins played with this club for three seasons, when he became a member of. the Warrington " A "team. After a sea- son's play, he was drafted in the first team, and with the exception of one season when he was " under a cloud " he has played with the team ever since.

Nevins has played in all positions in the team which has made him a very valuable member, in-as-much as he can place himself in any post in case of emergency. For instance, in the match last Saturday, which, by-the-bye, was Nevins' first turn out since his injury, he was placed in the three-quarters, during Jack Bate's sh rt cessation of play through injury. Latterly, howev r, his place has been almost oxclusively in the forwards, and he plays with characteristic energy and dash.

Of his play outside Warrington, Nevins has figured in two trial matches this season, and as a result he was chosen to play for Lancashire against Cheshire on November 4th. Unfortunately during the progress of the match, he was hurt, and this incapacitated him from playing until last Saturday, when he journeyed with the first team to Swinton, to play off their engage- ment in the Lancashire League competition. Now that he has got back to work, or rather play, it may be ex- pected that he will be one of the fifteen chosen to re[.te- sent the county. Football is the only athletic exercise Nevins indulges in, for he states that his spare time is too limited for other exercise.

THE REV. W. H. STABLES ON

PROFESSIONALIS M.

At the meeting of the Leeds and District Rugby Union on Thursday night last week, at the conclusion of the ordinary business Mr. C. Griff n moved, and Mr. Haigh seconded, a vote of thanks to the Rev. W. H. Stables for his services as chairman of the union for several years, and of regrets that they were about to lose his services.—Mr. Stables, in reply, said he was very sorry to leave them, but in accepting a curacy at Runcorn Parish Church he was doing that which he thought was fqr the greatest good. He trusted that the Union would prosper and continue to do its utmost to raise the game to a high moral level. The next two years would be very important years for Yorkshire football. In that time the great question of professionalism or no profes- sionalism in Rugby football would probably be definitely fought out, and therefore it behoved all wh9 were opposed to the introduction of the recognized profes- sional to be wide awake. He did not think that proft s- sionalism in Rugby football would ever become general in Yorkshire, although it was possible in the case of the richer clubs' bnt even here it would entail many disad- vantages. As for the great majority of Yorkshire clubs, professionalism was quite out of the question. They were altogether too poor, and, if wages were to be paid, they could not for one minute hope to compete with those of larger incomes. Their only way to maintain a reputation would be to preserve their amateur character and to compete in amateur cup-ties and competitions. The County Committee, as a corporate body, would doubtless hold on its way, and, if the worst came to the worst, we should but have professional and amateur Rugby football side by side. But he hoped and trusted that the evil day of professionalism in Rugby football would never coine.—" The Yorkshire Post."

PROPOSED SUPERANNUATION OF THE WORKHOUSE DOCTOR.

After the ordinary business had been transacted, at the Warrington Board of Guardians, on Wednes- day, the Board resolved itself into a Committee, to discuss the question of superannuating Dr. Spinks, the Workhouse Medical officer. It was we are given to understand, proposed by Mr. Heaton that Dr. Spinks be superannuated, and allowed two thiids of his present salary, which is £2OO alyear, This proposition having been seconded, Mr. A. J. Jolley proposed an amend- ment that the superannuation allowance should be £lOO a year. This was als3 seconded. After a considerable amount of discussion the matter was voted upon. There were nine votes recorded for the original mAion and five for the amendment. Upon being asked for his views Dr. Spinks said that he was willing to accept a superanauation of two thirds of his salary, but preferred to retain his appointment rather than accept the £lOO per annum. The question will be discussed at the Board meeting at the next fortnightly meeting.

TANNERS' LANE COAL WHARF,Isome part of God's Revelation which has been recorded WARRINGTON. no where else ? . Furthermore, do we possetgi,within the THE PEARSON AND KNOWLES COAL AND covers of the Bible, everything which was written by IRON COMPANY, LIMITED, Matthew. Mark, Luke, John, Peter, James, aertJude ? If we do not, what of their other productions? What, INCH HALL COLLIERIES, NEAR WIGAN. tan, of Andrew, James the elder, Philip, Bartholomew, LThomas, and Simon Zelotes ? Did they write nothing? OCAL MANAGER, ALFRED PERRIS. Again, in some parts of the Old Testament, the reader is All kinds of House Coal, Nuts, Burgy, and Engine Fuel referred to books that have been lost for ages. How does kept in stock. wagons pent to any Railway Station, House "Dissenter" purpose showing that those books were not orders promptly Attended to. inspired, or, that if inspired, they contained nothing of N.B.—Ord,r3 received also at 19a, Sankey-st. consequence which is not elsewhere recorded ? These are some of the difficulties he must effectually dispose of before he can take the first step towards demonstrating his thesis—viz., that no doctrine which is not set torth explicitly in Holy Writ is to be accepted as part of God's Revelation. Assuming that he does dispose of them, Ite must then prove that the Bible is the Word of God in its entirety—that is to say, he must show, not only that every truth recorded by inspiration has come down to us in writing, but that there is no revealed truth which has not so been recorded. Unless he can prove that every- thing which God hasmade known to the human race is contained in the Bible, he cannot logically assert that the Catholic Church, in this, that, or the other instance, proposes for our acceptance a doctrine which Grid has not revealed. So long as the doctrine in question does not contradict the Scriptures,he must at least allow that it may be part of Revelation. And even if undoubtedly it were not this, it would not follow of necessity that it is false, unless indeed it can be proved that the Al- mighty has revealed every truth concerning Himself and His Church and the world to come. But there is still another difficulty which our Dissent- ing friend must overcome before he can establish his position. Paving, as we will suppose made it clear that the entire Revelation given by God to man is con- tained in the Bible, he must enlighten us in regard to the interpretation of that most difficult of books. It is interpretated in various ways by various minds, but manifestly these conflicting interpretations cannot all express the meaning which lies at the bottom of the text. To press this matter well home, I will ask " Dissenter " whether he is certain that his own interpretation is the right one, and that, consequently, all who differ from him are in errer ? If he is, will he kindly explain how he arrived at this interesting state of mind? If he is not, then surely the interpretation which the Catholic Church puts on the Bible has, to sgy the least of it,quite as strong a claim to consideration as his own, and is no more certainly false than his is certainly true.— Obediently yours, AUG. J. HILTON. 31, Arthur•read, North Brixton, London, S.W., Dec. 12th, 1893. P.S.—lt is a sign of the times that a writer like "Dissen- ter," when speaking of Luther's connection with the Bible, can resist the temptation—a strong one, I have no doubt—to trot out the familiar threadbare fable that " the monk who shook the world "was the first to translate the Scriptures into German.
